# botdot API (for the Android app and other clients)

botdot's web page and the Android app use the same JSON API over HTTPS, through the machine's front door:
`https://<the machine's name>/api/…`.

**Who you are.** A machine holds one or more accounts, and each has its own botdot server ([ADR 028](adr/028-botdot-serves-several-accounts-on-one-machine-each-walled-off.md)). People reach only the front door. It signs them in with a passkey and keeps a session per app, and passes every other request of a session to that account's botdot server, which then answers as below. A request without a session never reaches a botdot server (see [The front door](#the-front-door)). The one exception is a job's call address, `https://<the machine's name>/call/<account>/<bot>/<job>`, which a device or service uses with the job's key (see A job that is called).

**Writes** (anything but `GET`) need the header `X-Bot-Dot: 1`. JSON bodies use `Content-Type: application/json`. Errors come back as `{ "error": "…" }` with a 4xx status, in words you can show the owner as they are.

**Start with `GET /api/version`.** Check `apiVersion` and `features` before using anything else. This page describes `apiVersion: 1`.

## The front door

The one program on a machine that people reach (`front-door.js`, [ADR 028](adr/028-botdot-serves-several-accounts-on-one-machine-each-walled-off.md)). It answers only as its own name (`FRONT_DOOR_ORIGIN`): any other `Host` is `403`. Its writes need `X-Bot-Dot: 1`, as botdot's do. Errors are `{ "error": "…" }`, a sentence for the person.

**Passkeys.** WebAuthn, with `attestation: "none"` and discoverable credentials, so signing in asks for no name: the passkey says whose account it is. Bytes travel as base64url text, in both directions: `challenge` and `user.id` in the options, and every field of `response` in what the page sends back, which is a `PublicKeyCredential` as `{ id, rawId, type, response: { clientDataJSON, attestationObject | authenticatorData, signature, userHandle } }`. A challenge works once, for 5 minutes.

**A session** is the cookie `botdot_session` (`HttpOnly`, `SameSite=Lax`, `Secure` on HTTPS), kept until it is signed out. Each sign-in makes one, for the app it names: `app` is `web`, `android` or `mac` (`web` when left out). The cookie stays with the front door and never reaches a botdot server.

**A sign-up link** is `https://<name>/sign-up/<token>`. It works once and ends after 7 days, and it may be for one account's name, which the page then shows and the sign-up keeps. Until the machine has an account, every start of the front door prints a new one, which makes the admin's own account (for `FRONT_DOOR_ADMIN`'s name, when it is set; not on a machine of one account, below). `node front-door.js sign-up-link [--account <name>]` prints another (the installer prints the admin's this way); it exits with `3` and "The account <name> exists already." when it does, and `2` for a name that isn't one.

- `GET /sign-up/<token>`: the sign-up page, which says that the admin of the machine can technically reach everything in the account (for the admin's sign-in link, the page that makes a new passkey: see The admin page). `410` with a page saying so for a link that was used, has ended, or never was.
- `POST /sign-up/<token>/options` with `{ name }`: `{ publicKey }`, the options for `navigator.credentials.create()`. `name` is the account's name: 2 to 24 lowercase letters, digits and dashes, starting with a letter (so that `botdot-<name>` is a Linux user's name), else `400`; another name than the link's own is `400` "This sign-up link is for the account <name>."; a name that is taken is `409`; a link that no longer works is `410`.
- `POST /sign-up/<token>` with `{ name, credential, app? }`: makes the account and its passkey, uses the link, and signs this app in: `201` with `{ ok: true, account }` and the session's cookie. A passkey that was not made for this sign-up, this site or this challenge is `400` and leaves the link working.
- `GET /sign-in`: the sign-in page. Until the machine has an account it says only "Open the link from your server's install."
- `POST /sign-in/options` with `{}`: `{ publicKey }`, the options for `navigator.credentials.get()`.
- `POST /sign-in` with `{ credential, app? }`: `200` with `{ ok: true, account }` and the session's cookie, or `401` with "That passkey didn't sign you in."
- **A native app's hand-off.** With `app: "android"` or `app: "mac"` and `handoff` (the SHA-256, base64url, of a secret the app keeps), `POST /sign-in` and `POST /sign-up/<token>` set no cookie: they answer `{ ok: true, account, code, next }`, where `next` is `botdot://signed-in?code=<code>`. A `handoff` with another `app` or none, or that is not 43 base64url characters, is `400`. The code works once, for 2 minutes, and is kept in memory only. The pages take the same from their address (`?app=android&handoff=…`), send the browser to `next` and show "Back to the app", a link to it, for a browser that opens no app without a tap.
- `POST /sign-in/handoff` with `{ code, verifier }`: the app's session (for the `app` the sign-in named). `200` with `{ ok: true, account }` and the session's cookie when the SHA-256 of `verifier` is the code's `handoff`; else `401` with "That sign-in didn't come from this app. Sign in again.", and the code is spent all the same. It counts toward the slowing down below.
- `POST /sign-out`: ends this session: `200` with `{ ok: true }`, and the cookie is cleared.
- `POST /sign-out-everywhere`: "Sign out everywhere" in Settings, on the web page and in the apps. Ends every session of this session's account, in every app, this one too: `200` with `{ ok: true, ended }` (how many ended), and the cookie is cleared. Without a session it is `401` with `signIn`, as below. No botdot server reaches it, so no tool of the Chief's or of a bot reads or ends a session.
- **Slowed down.** After 5 failed sign-ins, 5 hand-off codes that don't work, or 5 sign-up links that don't work, from one client, each next try waits twice as long as the one before, from 1 second up to 15 minutes: `429` with `Retry-After` (seconds) and "Too many failed sign-ins. Try again in … seconds." on the sign-in routes and the sign-up link's. A sign-in forgets them at once, an hour without one forgets them too. A client is the address that reached the front door, or, when that is the machine itself (Caddy or Tailscale Serve in front of it), the last address in `X-Forwarded-For`.
- `GET /front-door/session`: `{ admin, feedback, feedbackScreenshots, updates }`. `admin` is `true` when this session is the admin's (the account `FRONT_DOOR_ADMIN` names; never on a machine of one account), else `false`. The web page asks it behind a front door (`accounts` in features) and shows an "Admin" icon beside Settings that opens `/admin` only for `true`. `feedback` is `true` when Feedback goes to an admin page (a machine of several accounts with an admin; `false` on a machine of one account): the web page and the Android app show a "Feedback" icon beside Settings only then, and an older front door, which leaves it out, shows none. `feedbackScreenshots` is `true` when `POST /front-door/feedback` takes `screenshots` ([#223](https://github.com/Kordal/bot-dot/issues/223)): the apps show "Add screenshot" in the form only then, and send none to a front door that leaves it out. `updates` is `true` when this account's owner chooses when it switches to a new version ([#259](https://github.com/Kordal/bot-dot/issues/259), [ADR 053](adr/053-each-account-owner-chooses-when-their-account-switches-to-a-new-version.md)): a machine of several accounts whose root's step takes requests, for every account, the admin's own too ([#274](https://github.com/Kordal/bot-dot/issues/274)). The web page and the Android app show Updates in Settings only then, and an older front door, which leaves it out, shows none. Without a session it is `401` with `signIn`. No botdot server reaches it.
- `GET /front-door/update`: **when this account switches to a new version** (ADR 053): `{ autoUpdate, asked, canUpdate, ready, last }`. `autoUpdate` is the owner's "Update automatically", `true` unless they turned it off: then the admin's update leaves the account on the version it runs, with the new one already in its Docker. `ready` is that version while the account waits for it: `{ version, from, since, deadline }`, `version` the one it waits for and `from` the one it runs (source revisions, 7 characters, or `null`), `since` when it began to wait and `deadline` when the machine switches it without its owner (`since` and the admin's days; `null` otherwise). `last` is how its last switch went, `{ state, at, error? }`: `state` `switching`, `updated`, `kept` (it runs the version it ran, ADR 044) or `failed` (it runs none), `error` one line of at most 300 characters for the last two; a switch `switching` for 3 hours is `failed` with "The update didn't finish. Update now again."; `null` before root's step said anything of the account. `asked` is `true` while a switch waits for root's step, and `canUpdate` is `true` when "Update now" may be asked: the account waits, or its last switch didn't happen, and none is asked. Each field is what root's step wrote in `status/account-<name>.json`, checked field by field. A `GET` also asks for the switch of every account past its deadline. `404` wherever `updates` is `false`; without a session `401` with `signIn`.
- `POST /front-door/update` with `{ autoUpdate }` (`true` or `false`): the owner turns "Update automatically" on or off; the same answer. It applies from the admin's next update: turning it on doesn't switch an account that waits. `400` for anything but a boolean.
- `POST /front-door/update/now` with `{}`: **Update now.** `202` with the same answer, `asked` `true`. The machine is asked as for an account, with an empty file named `SWITCH-<name>`, which no account can be named: root's step switches this account alone to the version the front door's machine pins (the one the admin's update downloaded into it), with ADR 044's way back, under the installer's lock, so it never runs beside an update. An account that runs that version already, or isn't running, is left as it is. `409` "There is no new version to switch to." when `canUpdate` is `false`. Both POSTs answer `503` with `paused` for a suspended account, need `X-Bot-Dot: 1`, and reach no botdot server.
- `GET /front-door/update/notes`: **what the waiting version brings** (#303): `{ entries }`, the What's new notes (`{ id, title, points }`, as `GET /api/whats-new`) of the front door's version after the newest one the account's botdot server ships, newest first, at most 10. The front door asks that server's `GET /api/version` for its `whatsNew` over the account's socket, and sends nothing else. `entries` is empty while `ready` is `null`, and when the account's server doesn't say (one older than #303) or doesn't answer. The apps show the notes under Updates while a version waits; it marks nothing seen: the What's new pop-up after the switch shows as before. `404` wherever `updates` is `false`; without a session `401` with `signIn`.
- `POST /front-door/feedback` with `{ kind, text, screenshots? }`: **Feedback** ([#216](https://github.com/Kordal/bot-dot/issues/216), [ADR 048](adr/048-the-admin-page-shows-the-feedback-an-owner-sends-the-admin.md)), a bug (`kind` `bug`) or a feature request (`feature`) for the admin of this machine, sent from the icon beside Settings. `201` with `{ ok: true, id }`. `screenshots` ([#223](https://github.com/Kordal/bot-dot/issues/223)): at most 3, each `{ data }`, the picture's bytes as base64, at most 5 MB each, a PNG, JPEG or WebP; the front door reads the type from the bytes and ignores any type sent with them. All of it goes in this one request, so a report is never kept without its screenshots: this route takes a body of up to about 20 MB, every other front-door route 64 KB, and past it answers `413` "That is too much to send at once: at most 3 screenshots of 5 MB each go in one report." The text is still required. The front door keeps it for the admin page as `{ id, account, created, kind, text, app, version, screenshots? }`, `screenshots` `[{ type, size }]`, each picture a private file of its own beside the front door's data, never in it: the session's account and app (`web`, `android` or `mac`), and the version root's step last said (`null` before one). Nothing else of the account goes with it. `400` in plain words for another `kind`, an empty `text`, one over 2000 characters (trimmed), a fourth screenshot, one over 5 MB, or one that is no PNG, JPEG or WebP (an SVG, a GIF, text named `.png`); `429` "You sent 10 reports in the last hour. Try again later." for the 11th from one account within an hour. The front door keeps the newest 1000, and up to 500 MB of screenshots on the machine: past that the oldest reports' screenshots go first, and those reports keep their text with `screenshotsDeleted: true`. `503` with `paused` for a suspended account, as everything it asks. `404` on a machine of one account, or one without an admin. An account the admin deletes takes its reports and their screenshots with it. Without a session it is `401` with `signIn`. It needs `X-Bot-Dot: 1`, and no botdot server reaches it, so no Chief or bot sends or reads one.
- `GET /front-door/passkey.js`: the script of the two pages.
- **Installing from the browser** (issue #163): `GET` and `HEAD` of `/manifest.webmanifest`, the icons and screenshots it lists (`/icon.svg`, `/icon-192.png`, `/icon-512.png`, `/icon-maskable-512.png`, `/screenshot-wide.png`, `/screenshot-narrow.png`), `/apple-touch-icon.png` and `/offline.html` are answered by the front door itself, with or without a session: a browser fetches a manifest without its cookies, and Chrome and Edge offer Install only when it loads. They are the files of `public/`, the same in every account, read when the front door starts (`ETag`, `no-cache`). The sign-in page names the manifest too. Every other path still needs a session.
- `GET /.well-known/assetlinks.json`: the Android app (`dev.botdot.app`) with the SHA-256 fingerprints of its signing certificates, the Digital Asset Links Android's Credential Manager reads before it lets the app use this site's passkeys (the app's sign-in before it moved to the browser; kept for those installs): botdot's release key (the app on botdot's releases page) always, then each one in `FRONT_DOOR_ANDROID_KEYS` (`AA:BB:…`, comma separated). An entry that is no fingerprint is left out, and the front door says so when it starts.

**The admin page** is `/admin`, on the web only: the admin's session alone reaches it (the account `FRONT_DOOR_ADMIN` names, which the installer sets). Every other session gets `404` for `/admin` and everything under it, and none of it is passed to a botdot server; without a session `GET /admin` is a `302` to `/sign-in` and the rest `401`. It never shows what is inside an account.
- `GET /admin`: the page, which lists the accounts and the links, and does what follows, with `GET /front-door/admin.js` and its two fonts, `GET /front-door/fonts/Geist.ttf` and `GET /front-door/fonts/GeistMono.ttf` (the web page's own, from `public/fonts`).
- `GET /admin/links`: `{ links }`, every sign-up link and sign-in link, newest first: `{ id, created, expires, state, for?, signIn?, account?, usedAt?, revokedAt? }`. `id` is the link's SHA-256, which lets nobody in: the link itself is shown once, when it is made. `state` is `open`, `used` (`account` is the account it made, or gave a new passkey) or `ended` (it ran out, or the admin revoked it: `revokedAt`). `for` is the one account a link may make (the install's), or a sign-in link's account; `signIn` is `true` for a sign-in link.
- `POST /admin/links` with `{}`: a new sign-up link for anyone's account, `201` with `{ url, link }` (`link` as listed).
- `POST /admin/links/<id>/revoke`: the link makes no account (or passkey) from now on: `{ ok: true, link }`. `404` for no such link, `409` for one that was used or has ended.
- `GET /admin/accounts`: `{ accounts }`, by name: `{ name, created, lastSeen, state, admin?, share, ownShare, use, update? }`, and nothing else. `update` is what `GET /front-door/update` answers its owner, for every account, the admin's own too (ADR 053, #274); the admin page says on its card whether its owner updates it and what waits for it. `share` is the account's share of the machine (see Shares of the machine), `ownShare` `true` when it is its own and not the default, and `use` what root's step last measured of it, or `null`. `lastSeen` is when a session last reached the account (kept at most every 5 minutes; `null` before), `admin` is `true` for the admin's own. `state` is `running` (its botdot server answers on its socket), `suspended`, `making` (the machine is making it), `starting` (the machine is starting it again) or `stopped` (its botdot server doesn't answer). It comes from the front door's own data and a connection to the socket that sends nothing: no route of the admin page reaches a botdot server or returns anything from inside an account.
- `POST /admin/accounts/<name>/suspend` with `{}`: `{ ok: true }`. The account's owner gets the paused answer (below) at once, and the machine stops its botdot server, docker-helper and bots' computers by stopping its rootless Docker; its data, containers, Linux user, sessions and passkeys stay. `409` when it is suspended already, or is the admin's own account; `404` for no such account.
- `POST /admin/accounts/<name>/resume` with `{}`: `{ ok: true }`. The machine starts the account again as it was (brought up to the machine's version), and its sessions reach it again; until its botdot server answers, its pages are `503` "Your account is starting", and its `/api/…` `503` with `{ error, starting: true }`. `409` when it isn't suspended.
- `POST /admin/accounts/<name>/delete` with `{ name }`, the account's name typed again: `{ ok: true }`. Another `name`, or none, is `400` "Type the account's name, <name>, to delete it." and deletes nothing. The front door forgets the account, its passkeys, its sessions and its open sign-in links at once, and the machine erases its data, containers, images, volumes, socket folder, Linux user and group and sub-IDs. Its name can be taken again once the machine has erased it (`409` "That name is taken." until then). `409` for the admin's own account.
- `POST /admin/accounts/<name>/sign-in-link` with `{}`: a **sign-in link** for an account that exists, for an owner who lost every device: `201` with `{ url, link }`, where `url` is `https://<name>/sign-up/<token>`. It works once and ends after 1 day, or when revoked. Its page, `GET /sign-up/<token>`, says "Make a new passkey for your account, <name>."; `POST /sign-up/<token>/options` and `POST /sign-up/<token>` work as a sign-up's, with the account's own name (another is `400`), and the passkey is made for the account's own user handle. Made, it is the account's new passkey (its others stay), every other session of the account ends, and this app is signed in: `201` with `{ ok: true, account }`. `node front-door.js sign-in-link --account <name>` prints one on the machine, for the admin's own account too (exit `3` when there is no such account). `404` for no such account.

**Shares of the machine** ([#471](https://github.com/Kordal/bot-dot/issues/471)). Every account has a share: `{ cpu, ram, disk }`, whole numbers: CPUs (1 to 256), GB of RAM (2 to 4096) and GB of disk (12 to 100000). It has the default share (at first `{ cpu: 2, ram: 5, disk: 30 }`, with half the machine's RAM instead of 5 GB, at least 5, once root's step measured it: #126) until the admin gives it its own. Any other share is `400` "A share is whole numbers: 1 to 256 CPUs, 2 to 4096 GB of RAM and 12 to 100000 GB of disk." A share has no "computers at once" since [#108](https://github.com/Kordal/bot-dot/issues/108): one an older admin page sends with `computers`, or one kept before, is taken and handed out without it. A change is asked of the machine with the account's request (below), and root's step applies it: CPU and RAM as hard limits on the account's rootless Docker (its work slows down, near its RAM before anything is ended) and, in a file its docker-helper reads, as the limits of the account's one computer (ADR 035), from that computer's next start; and the disk as the size of the account's own disk, which grows at once and never shrinks (a smaller disk applies to an account made from then on). Nothing in an account shows its share or a limit.
- `GET /admin/share`: `{ share, machine }`: the default share, and the machine's load as root's step last measured it (every minute), `{ at, cpus, load, ramTotal, ramUsed, diskTotal, diskUsed }` (bytes; `load` the 1-minute load average), or `null` when nothing was measured in the last 5 minutes.
- `POST /admin/share` with `{ cpu, ram, disk }`: the default share, for every account without its own, each asked of the machine: `{ ok: true, share }`.
- `POST /admin/accounts/<name>/share` with `{ cpu, ram, disk }`, or `{ default: true }` to give it the default share again: `{ ok: true, share }`, the share it has now. `404` for no such account.
- An account's `use` in `GET /admin/accounts`: `{ at, cpu, ram, computers, diskSize, diskUsed, full? }`, measured from outside the account: `cpu` the CPUs it used in the last minute, `ram` its bytes of RAM less its file cache, which the kernel takes back when memory is short (as the machine's `ramUsed` is total less available; its shared memory, gVisor's, stays in, #126), `computers` 1 while the account's one computer runs and 0 while it does not (the page says "Its computer is on" or "off"), `diskSize` and `diskUsed` its disk's bytes, and `full: true` when less than 200 MB of it is free, which the page marks "Disk full": its runs fail with "No space left on the server." From 90% of its share's RAM the page marks "Near its RAM: its site may stall. Give it more RAM." (#126): under gVisor its sandboxes' file cache counts in that RAM, and at the share its disk is read again and again. `null` when nothing was measured of it in the last 5 minutes. Root's step writes it in files the front door reads (`FRONT_DOOR_LOAD`, `machine.json` and `account-<name>.json`); without them there is nothing.

**A suspended account.** Every request of its sessions is answered by the front door itself and reaches no botdot server: a page `503` that says only "Your account is paused by the admin." (and looks again every minute) for a `GET` of a page, `503` with `{ "error": "Your account is paused by the admin.", "paused": true }` for everything else, and a WebSocket `503`. Its owner can still sign in, sign out and sign out everywhere. A call to it is `502` "This account's botdot server isn't running. Try the call again later.", as for any account whose botdot server isn't running.

- `GET /admin/update`: `{ version, selfBuilt, asked, update, newest, days }`, what root's step last said: `version` is the version the front door and every account run (the image's source revision, 7 characters, or its id, 12; `null` before root's step said one), `selfBuilt` is `true` when the images were built on the machine, `asked` is `true` while an update waits for root's step, and `update` is the last update, `null` before any: `{ state, at, from, to, error?, accounts?, phase?, account?, log? }`, `state` `updating`, `updated` or `failed` (`error`, one line of the installer's, at most 500 characters, says why: a failed download ends with Docker's own reason, such as "no space left on device"). `accounts`, once the update ended, is each account's result (#194, ADR 044): `{ name, result, version?, error? }`, `result` `updated`, `kept` (it runs the version it ran, `version` when its image names one), `failed` (it runs no version), with `error`, one line of at most 300 characters, saying why, or `waiting` (ADR 053: its owner turned "Update automatically" off, and it runs the version it ran, `version` when its image names one, without `error`; a waiting account doesn't fail the update). One account that fails doesn't stop the others: the update ends `failed` naming the accounts kept back, and the page names each of them and why. An installer from before #194 writes no `accounts`. While `updating`, and when `failed`, `phase` is the phase the update is in or stopped in (#195): `check`, `clean`, `preflight`, `download`, `switch`, `front-door` or `done`, and `account` the account it was at, for `clean`, `preflight`, `download` and `switch`. A `failed` update has `log`: the last lines of the installer's `update.log`, at most 40, each one line of plain text of at most 300 characters. Each is left out when root's step said none (an older installer) or said it wrong. An update that has been `updating` for 3 hours is `failed` with "The update didn't finish. Update again.", in the phase it was in. `newest` is the newest signed release root's step found, `{ version, at }` (its source revision, 7 characters, and when it was found), `null` before it found one and for a self-built install: root's step looks once an hour at most, and an install or an update says the version it installed is the newest. The admin page shows "Version X is available" when it differs from `version`, else "This is the newest version." (#193) `days` is the deadline (ADR 053): an account whose owner turned "Update automatically" off waits at most this many days after the update that left it waiting (7 unless the admin set another).
- `POST /admin/update/days` with `{ days }`, a whole number from 1 to 60: the admin sets the deadline; the answer of `GET /admin/update`. It applies to the accounts that wait now too: one past the new deadline is asked to switch at once. `400` "The deadline is a whole number of days, 1 to 60." for anything else.
- `POST /admin/accounts/<name>/update` with `{}`: **Update now** for that account, as its owner's (ADR 053): `202` with `{ ok: true, update }`. `409` when the account has no version to switch to.
- `POST /admin/update` with `{}`: **Update every account.** `202` with the same answer, `asked` `true`. The machine is asked as for an account (below), with an empty file named `UPDATE`, which no account can be named. Root's step runs the installer it was installed with as an update (`install.sh --update`, which asks nothing and stops at anything it would have to ask): the newest signed release of the repositories the install pinned, checked before anything is downloaded (when the front door and every running account run its very images already, it changes nothing and restarts nothing, and the update is `updated` with `from` equal to `to`: "It was the newest already", #193), or, for a self-built install, the images built on the machine (the admin pulls the source and runs `make images` first). It runs in phases of its own, never the install's questions ([ADR 045](adr/045-the-update-runs-in-phases-of-its-own-apart-from-the-install.md)): it checks the new release, makes room and checks each account's disk, downloads the new images into the machine's Docker and every account's before it restarts any (the bots' computer image only into an account that has none: an account's computer gets its new image when it is updated or reset, #191), then moves each account and last the front door onto them; a suspended account stays stopped. An account whose owner turned "Update automatically" off is left `waiting` on the version it runs when it ran the version the front door pinned until now, or waits already for this very one: its old images stay, and its owner or the deadline switches it later (`POST /front-door/update/now`). One that would be two versions behind is switched with the others, so no account runs more than one version behind the front door. The update finds nothing new when every running account runs the newest images or waits for them. An account whose download fails isn't restarted, and one whose new botdot server doesn't answer within about 2 minutes runs its old version again (ADR 044): both stay on the version they ran, and the others are updated. The images no Docker uses any more go (`lib/image-cleanup.js`; `update.cleaned` in each account's audit log), and also before each Docker's download, so that a full disk makes room instead of stopping every update (#190). Before anything is downloaded, each running account's disk must have room for the new server image (#192): the layers it lacks, as the registry's manifest of the signed image gives them, three times over and 1 GB more. An account short of it stops the update with `error` "The account NAME needs X GB free for this update and has Y GB. …", before anything is downloaded into an account or restarted (the machine's own Docker may have the new server image already: root's copy of the installer downloads it before it hands over, ADR 036); when the registry's answer gives no sizes, the update goes on without the check. The front door restarts during it: the page asks `GET /admin/update` again until it answers, and says the `phase` in words ("Downloading for kordal…"); after a failure it says where it stopped and shows the `log`. `409` "This machine can't be updated from here. Run its installer again." when the front door has no requests folder.
- `GET /admin/feedback`: `{ reports }`, the owners' Feedback, newest first: `{ id, account, created, kind, text, app, version, screenshots?, screenshotsDeleted?, doneAt? }`, `screenshots` `[{ id, type, size }]` (`id`: its number in the report, from 0). The admin page draws each report's text as text, never as markup, and each screenshot as a thumbnail that opens it at full size.
- `GET /admin/feedback/<id>/screenshot/<n>`: the report's screenshot `n`, its bytes with the type the front door read from them (`image/png`, `image/jpeg` or `image/webp`), `Content-Disposition: inline`, `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff` and a `sandbox` CSP, so it is never drawn as a page. `404` for no such report or screenshot, and for every session but the admin's.
- `POST /admin/feedback/<id>/done` with `{}`: the report stays, marked done: `{ ok: true, report }`. `POST /admin/feedback/<id>/delete` with `{}`: it is gone, with its screenshots: `{ ok: true }`. Both `404` for no such report.

**The machine is asked** for every change of an account by the same request as a new account (below): an empty file named as the account. Root's step reads what the front door's data says of that account (there, or suspended, or deleted) and makes the machine match: makes it, starts it again, stops it, or erases it. It erases only an account the front door's data names as deleted, and never the admin's own; an update of the install leaves a suspended account stopped.

**A new account** made from a sign-up link is asked of the machine: the front door writes an empty file named as the account in `FRONT_DOOR_REQUESTS`, and root's step (`install.sh --requested`, which systemd starts when that folder isn't empty) makes its Linux user, rootless Docker under gVisor, botdot server and docker-helper as the install made the admin's, and removes the file. The install's own account has its botdot server already and is asked for by nobody. Until the new botdot server answers, the account's pages are `503` with a page that says "Your account is being made" and looks again by itself, and its `/api/…` is `503` with `{ "error": "Your account is being made…", "making": true }` and `Retry-After`. The new account starts with its own setup page: its own Claude sign-in and time zone (the machine's until it picks one).

**The Android app** signs in on the front door's own pages, in the phone's browser (a Custom Tab, `androidx.browser`), the way the Mac app does: the browser and the password manager used there (Proton Pass, Bitwarden, Google Password Manager) answer the passkey for the site itself, so neither `assetlinks.json` nor `FRONT_DOOR_ANDROID_KEYS` takes part, and any domain works. The app makes a secret (32 random bytes, base64url), keeps it in its private storage, and opens `/sign-in?app=android&handoff=<its SHA-256>`; after the passkey the page sends the browser to `botdot://signed-in?code=…`, which Android hands to the app (its main activity, `singleTask`, closes the tab). The app sends the code and its secret to `POST /sign-in/handoff`, forgets the secret, and keeps the session's cookie on the phone. Another app may claim `botdot://` and see the code, never the secret, so it signs nobody in. A front door older than this answers the page with `400`, which the page shows. The app sends the cookie with every request, the live feed, pictures and a bot's screen included; a `401` with `signIn` sends it back to its sign-in screen. Earlier versions of the app signed in through Android's Credential Manager with `app: "android"` and the origin `android:apk-key-hash:<the certificate's SHA-256, base64url>`; the front door still takes that for botdot's release key and each fingerprint in `FRONT_DOOR_ANDROID_KEYS`, and for no other.

**The Mac app** signs in on the front door's own pages, in a browser sheet (AuthenticationServices' `ASWebAuthenticationSession`, ephemeral), not with Apple's native passkey sheet: that one needs the app signed with a developer team and an associated domain, which an app built ad hoc for the owner's Mac has not, and which `localhost` cannot be. The app makes a secret, opens `/sign-in?app=mac&handoff=<its SHA-256>` (or with a local botdot server's setup key, `/sign-up/<setup key>?app=mac&handoff=…`), and the page, after the passkey, sends the sheet to `botdot://signed-in?code=…`, which ends the sheet. The app then sends the code and its secret to `POST /sign-in/handoff` and keeps the session's cookie, per botdot address, in a file only its user can read. It sends it with every request, the live feed, files and a bot's screen included; a `401` with `signIn` sends it back to its sign-in screen. Its `clientDataJSON` comes from the browser, so its origin is the site's own.

**A machine of one account** (`FRONT_DOOR_ONE_ACCOUNT=<name>`): the Mac app's local botdot server ([ADR 028](adr/028-botdot-serves-several-accounts-on-one-machine-each-walled-off.md), `compose.mac.yaml`). No sign-up link is printed at start, `node front-door.js sign-up-link` refuses, and a stored one opens nothing (`410`). The one account's passkey is made with `FRONT_DOOR_SETUP_KEY`, a secret of 32 to 100 base64url characters that only the Mac app holds: `/sign-up/<setup key>` works as a sign-up link until the account exists, for that account: its page shows the name, which cannot be changed. Without a setup key no account can be made. There is no admin page.

**Calls.** `/call/<account>/<botId>/<jobId>` is a job's call address ([ADR 026](adr/026-a-call-is-let-in-on-the-jobs-key-alone.md)): it needs no session and no `X-Bot-Dot`, and is passed to that account's botdot server as `/api/bots/<botId>/jobs/<jobId>/call`, the method and body unchanged, where the job's key lets it in or not (see A job that is called for the answers). The botdot server is sent only `Authorization`, `X-Botdot-Signature-256`, `X-Botdot-Timestamp` and `X-Hub-Signature-256` (a signed call's), `Content-Type`, `Content-Length`, `Transfer-Encoding`, `User-Agent` and `Host` of what the sender wrote, the account in `X-Botdot-Account`, and the client (as below) in `X-Forwarded-For`: no cookie, no Chief's or run's key, no Tailscale login. So a call is let in from wherever the front door can be reached: the tailnet's devices when the machine is on a tailnet only, anything on the internet when it is public.
- A session's own request to `/api/bots/<botId>/jobs/<jobId>/call` is `404`: a call goes to its call address, where it is counted.
- An account that does not exist is `401` with "Wrong key.", as a wrong key: a sender without the key learns nothing. A path under `/call/` that is no call address is `404`. When the account's botdot server isn't running the answer is `502` with "This account's botdot server isn't running. Try the call again later."
- **Slowed down.** Every `401` (no key, a wrong key, a missing, wrong or too old signature, a job that can't be called, an account that does not exist) counts against the client as a failed sign-in does, in a count of its own: after 5, each next call from that client waits twice as long as the one before, from 1 second up to 15 minutes, `429` with `Retry-After` and "Too many calls with a wrong key. Try again in … seconds.", the right key included, and reaches no botdot server. A call that is let in forgets nothing, so that one job's key can't clear the guesses at another's; an hour without a wrong key forgets them. The count of sign-ins is apart: a sender's wrong keys don't slow down its owner's sign-in.

**Without a session**, `GET /api/version` is the front door's own `{ "app": "bot-dot", "frontDoor": true, "features": ["accounts", "passkeys"] }`, so that an app can tell a front door from an older botdot server before it signs in. Every other `/api/…` is `401` with `{ "error": "Sign in first.", "signIn": "/sign-in" }`, which the web page answers by opening the sign-in page; any other `GET` is a `302` to `/sign-in`.

**With a session**, everything else, WebSockets too, is passed to the account's botdot server, paths unchanged. The front door names the account to it and drops `X-Chief-Key`. When that botdot server isn't running the answer is `502` with "Your account's botdot server isn't running…", or `503` while the machine is still making the account (above). A session reaches its own account and no other: an `X-Botdot-Account` it sends is replaced, and `/admin` is the front door's own.

**Behind the front door.** A botdot server takes its account's owner only on its socket (`BOT_DOT_FRONT_DOOR_SOCKET`; the front door opens `<FRONT_DOOR_SOCKETS>/<account>/botdot.sock`), and only when the front door names its own account (`BOT_DOT_ACCOUNT`) in `X-Botdot-Account`; another account, or none, is `403`. On its port, which the front door never uses, it takes the Chief's tools, the bots' run tools with their run's key, and `GET /api/version` for the image's healthcheck: anything else is `403` "botdot is reached through its front door.", and a call there, whoever sends it, `403` "A call goes to its address at the front door, /call/<account>/<bot>/<job>." The socket itself is `0666`, so that the front door's user can open it across the account's rootless Docker; what keeps everyone else out is its folder, which only the server's user and the front door may enter. A botdot server whose socket folder everyone may enter makes no socket and says why in its log. The owner checks of a single-owner botdot are gone: `BOT_DOT_ALLOWED_USERS`, `BOT_DOT_OWNER_KEY`, `POST /api/owner/link` and `/owner-login`.

## Version

`GET /api/version`
```json
{ "app": "bot-dot", "version": "0.1.0", "revision": "659cb5e…", "short": "659cb5e",
  "apiVersion": 1, "features": ["events", "uploads", "setup", "updates", "jobs-pause", "chief",
  "vault-requests", "bitwarden", "memory", "bot-messages", "chief-suggestions", "backups", "android-app", "inbox", "mac-app", "figure", "outfits", "unread", "opening-line", "working-line", "ratings", "tools", "vault-types", "needs-text", "message-key", "ask-continues", "connector-waits", "message-waits", "ended-by", "job-line", "asks-you", "decline-words", "staged-changes", "proposal-carries-on", "memory-chips", "memory-cards", "memory-forget", "change-summary", "groups", "checkup", "job-calls", "self-built", "accounts", "passkeys", "vault-in-computer", "chief-skills", "vault-ssh-keys", "chief-name", "claude-api-key", "vault-switch", "chief-action", "web-push", "job-notify", "job-call-signed", "computer-reset", "old-computer-copy", "computer-usage", "group-all", "vault-remove", "vault-ssh-key-add", "vault-github-app", "vault-files", "steer", "chief-steer", "reply-draft", "chief-ssh-key-add", "chief-ssh-key-change", "chief-vault-remove", "chief-connectors", "whats-new", "notes-wake", "vault-file", "override", "document-viewer", "chat-attachments", "more-always", "plan", "pins-shared", "workspace-files", "token-usage"], "docker": true, "whatsNew": "2026-10-22-zzzz-token-usage" }
```

`whatsNew`: the id of the newest What's new note this version ships (`null` when it ships none), so that the front door can say what a waiting update brings (`GET /front-door/update/notes`, #303).

`accounts` and `passkeys`: this botdot server is one account on its machine, reached through a front door that signs in with passkeys (see [The front door](#the-front-door)). Without a session the front door answers this route itself, with both.

### What's new

When `features` has `whats-new` ([#219](https://github.com/Kordal/bot-dot/issues/219)): the notes of the releases this account has not seen, which the web page and the Android app show once after an update, in a pop-up with "Got it".

- `GET /api/whats-new`: `{ entries: [{ id, title, points }] }`, at most 3, newest first. A note is a file of `whats-new/` in the image, `<YYYY-MM-DD-short-name>.md`: `id` is its name, `title` its first line, `points` its 2 to 4 lines starting with `- `. Plain text: the apps draw it as text, never as markup. An account's server that starts without a seen id (a new account, or one whose server just got `whats-new`) takes every note up to then as seen, so it gets `{ "entries": [] }`.
- `POST /api/whats-new/seen` with `{ id }`: every note up to `id` is seen, for every app of the account: `{ ok: true }`. An `id` older than what was seen changes nothing; one that is not the name of a note there is, `400`. The app sends the newest note it showed when the pop-up is closed, any way it is closed.

The account keeps the id of the newest note it has seen in `data/whats-new.json`, so a restored backup brings it back. The Chief's tools reach neither route.

## The live feed

`GET /api/events` is Server-Sent Events: one connection for everything that happens. The Android app keeps it open in a foreground service, which is where its notifications come from. A browser keeps nothing open: the server follows this feed for it and sends what notifies through the browser's push service (see [Notifications on this device](#notifications-on-this-device)).

- **The first message** is `event: hello`. It gives where things stand: the version fields above, plus `lastEventId`, `running` (runs going on now), `chiefBusy`, `chiefName` (with `chief-name` in `features`: the Chief's name, see The Chief's name), `claude` (`{ loggedIn }` as the sign-in check says it; when the check could not tell, `{ loggedIn: false }` while the server holds jobs and messages as signed out, and `null` otherwise: see A check that could not tell), `loginRequests`, `bitwarden` (`{ configured, unlocked, wantedBy }`), `approvals` (`{ jobs: [...], tiles: [...], memory: n, memoryChanges: [...] }`), `asks` (as in the `asks` event), `plans` (ids of Chief chats whose bot plan still waits for an answer) and `update` (`{ available }`). It has no id.
- **Every other event** has an `id:` like `mufpb2z3.17`. Remember the last one, and reconnect with the header `Last-Event-ID: <id>`, or `?since=<id>`, to get the events you missed. The server keeps its last 500 events for that. Of the changes of a working line (`working_line`, `group_working_line`) it keeps only the newest of each run, so a line that changes every second does not push the other events out: what you are replayed holds each run's line once, as it read last (see The working line).
- **`event: reset`** means the missed events can't be replayed: botdot restarted (after an update, say) or you were away too long. Reload everything, then carry on with the feed.
- **Keep-alive:** a `: ping` comment arrives every 20 seconds. `retry: 5000` asks clients to wait 5 seconds before reconnecting.

| Event | Data | Typical use |
|---|---|---|
| `run_started` | a run summary (below) | show "working…" |
| `run_finished` | a run summary, with `status`, `result` (first 300 characters) and `error`; `passedOn: true` when its reply went to a Chief chat (that `chief_reply` notifies instead); `new: true` when it is a new reply (see New replies) | notification: "📰 News: Found 3 articles." |
| `chief_started` | `{ chatId, title }` | |
| `chief_reply` | `{ chatId, title, status, text, error, costUsd }`: `status` is `"success"`, `"error"` (the message has `error` and was not stopped, whatever `text` it has) or `"stopped"` (the owner stopped it; a reply that a sign-out or the time limit ended is an `"error"`; see A failed reply); and `from` (a bot) when a bot's own message was posted in that chat (a file it sent, or its reply in place of a Chief turn that failed; a Chief reply about a bot's result has no `from` here, see A late result); `files` when the message has them; `unasked: true` when the Chief wrote it without being asked (see The Chief writes first); `new: true` when it is a new reply | notification, titled with `from.name` when there is one |
| `chief_suggestion` | `{ chatId, title, from, text }` | notification: a bot's suggestion waits in a new Chief chat |
| `plan_closed` | `{ chatId }` | the plan in that chat was approved, declined or built: dismiss its notification |
| `login_request` | `{ id, kind: "login" \| "card", botId, botName, botColor, site, name, from, amount }` | high-priority notification; open the login request screen |
| `login_request_closed` | `{ id, status }` | dismiss that notification |
| `bitwarden` | `{ configured, unlocked, wantedBy }`; `wantedBy` (`{ id, name, color }`) while a bot waits for you to unlock it | "Bitwarden is locked" |
| `approvals` | `{ jobs: [{ botId, botName, botEmoji, botColor, jobId, jobName, kind, when, text, yesOn? }], tiles: [{ tileId, title, botId, botName, botColor, by, text, yesOn }], memory: n, memoryChanges: [{ id, action, botId, botName, botColor, text, at }] }`. With `memory-cards` in `features`, a memory change also has `title`, `meta`, `yes` and `no`, and a connection opened with `?guarded=1` is sent the guarded ones too, each with `guarded: true` and counted in `memory` (see Memory, The contract). With `needs-text` in `features`, a job and a tile have `text` and can have `yesOn: "card"` (see What a notification says). With `ask-continues`, one the Chief asked for in a chat can have `then` (see What follows a yes). With `staged-changes`, a change to an approved job or tile is an entry too, with `change: true`, `no` and `yes` (see A change that waits) | one notification each, with Decline / Approve, or the entry's `no` and `yes`; show `text` under the bot's name; with `yesOn: "card"`, Decline alone, and a tap opens Needs you |
| `asks` | `{ waiting: n, list: [{ id, kind: "permission" \| "skill" \| "tool" \| "bot-change" \| "connector-action" \| "vault-switch" \| "vault-remove" \| "override" \| "chief-action", botId, botName, botColor, what, skill, text, at }] }`: permissions and skills bots ask for, tools the Chief asks to install for a bot, changes to a bot and actions of a connected app that wait for the owner. A `tool` ask also has `tool` (its name). `text` is what the notification says under the bot's name: "Asks to install gh", or "Asks to install stripe from a link"; with `needs-text` in `features`, an ask of every kind has it (see What a notification says). An entry can have `yesOn: "card"`: the owner's yes is given on the ask's card only (a tool from a link, a changed bot, a connected-app action and a skill a bot wrote; see Things that need the owner). With `ask-continues` in `features`, an entry can have `then` (see What follows a yes), and a `bot-change` entry has `by`, `chatId` when the Chief asked from a chat, `title` and `parts` (see A changed bot). A `bot-change` entry can have `yes` (see A changed bot). With `connector-waits`, a `connector-action` entry has `title`, `rows` in their short form, with `more` where they were cut, `held` and, when its run no longer waits, `stoppedAt` (see An action in a connected app) | one notification each, with Decline / Allow (or Install); show `text` where there is one; with `yesOn: "card"`, Decline alone, and a tap opens Needs you |
| `asks`, with `botId` | `{ botId, asks }`: that bot's open question changed (see A bot that asks). `asks` is `{ text, runId, at }`, or `null` when the question ended. The event has the name of the one above and is told apart by its fields: this one has `botId` and no `list`, that one `list` and no `botId`. An app from before `asks-you` reads `list`, finds none and changes nothing. With `asks-you` in `features` | set or clear "Asks: …" on that bot's line; never a notification: the run's `run_finished` notifies |
| `dashboard` | `{ tile }`: one tile's new value (the tile as in `GET /api/dashboard`), or `{ changed: true }`: tiles were added, changed or removed | update that tile, or load the Board again |
| `update_available` | `{}` | |
| `claude` | `{ loggedIn }`, when Claude's sign-in changed. With `job-line` in `features` it is sent when the server sees the change, also while no app is connected: an app that connects again gets it with what it missed, and `hello.claude` says where it stands. It is sent only for a check that could tell: one that could not sends nothing, and after a `hello` whose check could not tell the next check that can tell is sent also when nothing changed. One exception: a sign-out whose check could not tell is sent as `{ loggedIn: false }`, because the server holds jobs and messages as signed out from then on (see A check that could not tell) | "Claude isn't signed in" |
| `opening_line` | `{ chat: "bot:<id>", botId, runId, text }` for a bot's run, `{ chat: "chief:<chatId>", chatId, text }` for the Chief: what it said it is about to do (see The opening line) | show `text` under that chat and as the bot's preview, until that run's `run_finished` or that chat's `chief_reply`; never a notification |
| `working_line` | `{ chat: "bot:<id>", botId, runId, text }`: what the line of a bot's running run reads now, each time it changes, at most once a second per run (see The working line). With `working-line` in `features` | show `text` under that chat and as the bot's preview, in place of what was there, until that run's `run_finished`; never a notification |
| `run_waits` | `{ chat: "bot:<id>", botId, runId, waitingFor: "ok" }` when a bot's run begins to wait for the owner's answer on an action in a connected app, and `{ chat: "bot:<id>", botId, runId }`, without `waitingFor`, when it no longer waits (see An action in a connected app). With `connector-waits` in `features` | show "Waiting for your OK" as that run's line, until the event without `waitingFor` or that run's `run_finished`; never a notification |
| `run_steered` | `{ chat: "bot:<id>", botId, runId, steered }`: a run read a message the owner steered into it, `steered` being the run's whole list (see Steer). With `steer` in `features` | read the bot's runs again: the message stands in that run; never a notification |
| `chief_steered` | `{ chat: "chief:<chatId>", chatId, steered }`: the Chief's reply that is going read a message the owner steered into it, `steered` being that reply's whole list (see Steer in a Chief chat). With `chief-steer` in `features` | read the chat again: the message stands before that reply; never a notification |
| `queued` | `{ chat: "bot:<id>", botId, queued }`: the owner's messages that wait for that bot, the whole list as it is now, whenever it changes: a message is taken, starts, or is taken back (see A message that waits). For a Chief chat `{ chat: "chief:<chatId>", chatId, queued }` (see The Chief takes a message while it replies). With `message-waits` in `features` | draw them at the end of that chat, faded; never a notification |
| `unread` | `{ chat: "bot:<id>" \| "chief:<chatId>", unread, firstUnread, readAt }`: that chat's new replies changed (a new one came, or the chat was read: `unread: 0`) | set that chat's dots; at 0, remove its notification |
| `rating` | `{ chatId, index, rating: "up" \| "down" \| null }`: the owner rated an unasked message of that Chief chat, or cleared its rating (see The Chief writes first) | fill or clear 👍 and 👎 under that message |
| `checkup` | `{ on, days, time, webSearch, when, timeZone, lastRanAt }`: the checkup's setting changed, in an app or by the Chief (see The Chief's checkup). With `checkup` in `features` | show the switches and the line as they now are |
| `chief_name` | `{ name }`: the Chief was given another name, in setup, in an app or by asking it (see The Chief's name). With `chief-name` in `features` | show `name` wherever the Chief is named; never a notification |
| `chat_seen` | `{ chat: "bot:<id>" \| "chief:<chatId>" }`: an app opened that chat. For apps from before `unread`; with `unread` in `features`, follow `unread` instead | dismiss that chat's notification |
| `bot_message` | `{ from, to, priority, delivered: "started" \| "queued" \| "inbox", chainId, hop, preview }` | optional: "🏄 Web Surfer → 📬 Mail Assistant" |
| `groups` | `{}`: a group was made, changed or deleted, or a deleted bot left one (see Groups). With `groups` in `features` | read `GET /api/group-chats` again |
| `group_run_started`, `group_run_finished` | a run summary with `groupId`, `groupMessageId` and `chat: "group:<id>"`: a turn in a group started or ended (see Groups). Never `run_started` or `run_finished`. `group_run_finished` has `notify: false` for a reply and, for one that asks the owner something, `asks`, `title` and `question`, with `notify: true` unless an app showed the group as it arrived (see A card and a question in a group); `new: true` for a new reply in the group, and `passed: true` with `quiet: true` and an empty `result` for a pass | never draw it in the bot's own chat; ring only for `notify: true`, and open the group |
| `group_message` | `{ chat: "group:<id>", groupId, message }`: the owner's message in a group as it is now (see A group's chat): when it was taken, and whenever whom it waits for changes | draw it, and whom it waits for; never a notification |
| `group_message_gone` | `{ chat: "group:<id>", groupId, id }`: the owner took that message back | remove it |
| `group_asks` | `{ chat: "group:<id>", groupId, asks }`: a group's open question changed, `asks` being `{ botId, botName, text, runId, at }` or `null` (see A card and a question in a group). Never `asks`, which an app from before groups acts on | "Asks" on the group's row |
| `group_limit` | `{ chat: "group:<id>", groupId, limit }`: a line of the group stopped at the limit of 12 replies, went on or was ended, or who waits changed, `limit` being `{ at, replies, waits, messages, text }` or `null` (see The limit) | the line "The group stopped" with "Go on" at the end of the group's chat, or its removal; never a notification |
| `group_unread` | `{ chat: "group:<id>", groupId, unread, firstUnread, readAt }`: a group's new replies, whenever the count changes. Never `unread`, which an app from before groups acts on | the dots on the group and its "New" line; `unread: 0` reads it in every app |
| `group_opening_line`, `group_run_waits` | as `opening_line` and `run_waits`, for a turn in a group: with `groupId`, and `chat: "group:<id>"` in place of the bot's chat | that turn's line, in the group |
| `group_working_line` | as `working_line`, for a turn in a group: `{ chat: "group:<id>", groupId, botId, runId, text }`, each time that turn's line changes (see The working line) | that member's line, in the group and in the group's row |

Every event's data also has `at`, when it happened.

**A run summary:** `{ id, botId, botName, botEmoji, botColor, kind: "ai" | "script", trigger, jobName, status: "running" | "success" | "error" | "stopped" | "interrupted", startedAt, endedAt, costUsd, result, error }`. A turn in a group also has `groupId` and `groupMessageId`, and `passed: true` when it ended as a pass (see Groups). With `ended-by` in `features`, a run that was ended has `endedBy` (see Who ended it). With `asks-you`, a run whose reply asks the owner something has `asks: true`, `title` and `question` (see A bot that asks).

## Bots

- `GET /api/bots`: every bot, with `id, name, emoji, color, description, group, pinned, order, jobs[], nextRunAt, running, queued, computer, state, …`. `queued`, when `features` has `message-waits`: the owner's messages that wait for the bot (see A message that waits). `state` is what its face shows: `idle`, `working`, `waiting` (it asked teammates and waits for their answers), `needs_you` (something of it waits for the owner's OK, or, with `asks-you` in `features`, it asks them something: see A bot that asks), `failed` or `paused`. Draw a state you don't know as the definition's `default` state. Fetch the bots again on the `waiting` event too.
- `GET /api/figure`: the one head every app draws bots from, when `features` has `figure` ([ADR 003](adr/003-one-figure-definition-for-all-apps.md), which says exactly how to draw and move it). `{ version, box: [w, h], head: { cx, cy, r }, tones: { white, ink }, pivot: { head: [x, y], eyes: [x, y] }, attach: { hat: { at: [x, y], scale } }, kit, chief, small, default, states: { <state>: { eyes: [shape], pupils: [shape], pose, opacity?, move?: { seconds, easing, keys: [{ at, head?, eyes?, pupils? }] } } } }`. A bot is the `head` circle in its colour, with nothing behind it, the face of its `state` and its hat. A face is two lists of shapes in the box's units (the shapes of a hat, below, with `fill` and `stroke` each `"main"`, `"deep"`, `"white"` or `"ink"`; `tones` gives white and ink as `#rrggbb`): `eyes`, and `pupils`, which look around. There are three layers, one inside the other: `head` (the circle, the face and the hat), `eyes` (the face) and `pupils`. A `pose` places them: `head: { move: [x, y], turn }`, `eyes: { move, scale: [x, y] }`, `pupils: { move }`; `turn` is degrees clockwise about `pivot.head`, `scale` is about `pivot.eyes`, and what is left out is no move, no turn and a scale of 1. `pose` is the state's still face (reduced motion). `move` loops for ever: each key is the pose with the key's values over it, at time `at` (0 to 1), with `easing` (`linear` or `ease-in-out`) between each pair of keys, every number on its own. `opacity` fades the whole mark. `version` is the format's (2; 1 was a stick figure that was never released): use the definition only if you know its `version`, ignore keys you don't know, and keep a copy in the app for a server without `figure`; with no definition you know, draw one bare head with two round eyes. `small: { view: [x, y, width, height] }` is the mark that stands for a bot beside its name in running text, and for each thing that waits in "Needs you": the head's circle with the bot's hat, without eyes, in the bot's colour, drawn so that the part `view` of the box fills the mark. It never moves. For a definition without `small`, show the whole box.
- **A bot's outfit**, when `features` has `outfits` ([ADR 004](adr/004-pieces-as-restricted-shape-lists.md)). An outfit is a hat. Every bot has `outfit`: `null` (it was never given one: draw a bare head) or `{ hat }`: the name of a hat in the figure definition's `kit`, a drawn hat (below), or `""` for none. Set it with `POST /api/bots` or `PUT /api/bots/:id`: `{ "outfit": { "hat": "cap" } }`. `""` takes the hat off, and `{ "hat": "" }` means "no hat, on purpose", which is not `null`. `""` is the only way to say "none": a hat sent as `null` is a hat left out, so it stays as it was, and a bot never given an outfit stays at `null`. A name the kit lacks is refused (`400`, with the valid names in `error`), and so is any key other than `hat` (`400`: `An outfit has no "holds". Its only key is "hat", …`): bots hold nothing. The bot keeps its hat, and nothing else of that request is kept either. A bot that was stored with a held object by the stick-figure build (never released) is read without error and returned as `{ hat }`; the object is gone from the server's data the next time the bots are saved. One that held something and wore no hat is returned as `null`, as never given an outfit, so the server gives it a hat (below). A changed outfit comes with the `bots` event, like any change to a bot. A client draws a hat it doesn't have in its definition as nothing, and the head as usual. There is no picker in any app: the Chief of Staff sets the hat (`create_bot`, `update_bot`). A bot left at `null` (one from before outfits, or one made without) is given a hat by the server itself, in the background: a kit hat Claude picks from its name and task, usually within a minute or two of the server's start or of the bot's creation. When Claude can't be reached the bot stays `null` and the server tries again with a growing wait (up to an hour), and at once when it sees Claude signed in again; a bot for which Claude keeps naming hats the kit lacks stays `null` until the server starts again. The hat arrives with the `bots` event and nothing else is sent about it: no run, no chat, no notification. A bot whose outfit was ever set, also to no hat, is never dressed this way; the one exception is the stored bot above that only held something.
- **A drawn hat.** When the kit has no hat that fits a bot, the Chief of Staff draws one, and `hat` carries it in place of a name: `{ "outfit": { "hat": { "shapes": [ { "shape": "circle", "cx": 5, "cy": 3, "r": 2, "fill": "deep" } ] } } }`. A string is always a kit name and an object always a drawn hat. It is returned with the bot exactly as it was stored, and kept as it is when the bot's colour changes: a shape names a tone, not a colour. Its shapes are those of a kit hat (next point), and the server checks each limit before it stores the hat:
  - only `shapes` in the piece (no `means`), 1 to `kit.limits.shapes` (6) of them;
  - a shape is `rect`, `circle`, `line` or `path`, with only the fields of its kind, and every number a finite JSON number;
  - a `path`'s `d` is a list of 2 to `kit.limits.commands` (16) commands, `M` `L` `Q` `C` `Z` with absolute points, the first its only `M`; never a string;
  - every point, control points included, lies inside the hat's box: 0 to 10 across and 0 to 6 down (`kit.hat.box`); the whole of a `rect` and the whole of a `circle` lie inside it too; a `rect` has `w` and `h` above 0 and `r` up to half its shorter side, a `circle` an `r` above 0;
  - `fill` and `stroke` are `"main"` or `"deep"`, and a shape has at least one; a `stroke` has a `width` from 0.8 to 2 (`kit.limits.stroke`), and only a stroke has one; a `line` has a stroke and no fill.

  A hat that breaks a limit is refused: `400`, with the one reason in `error` (for example `The hat you drew can't be worn: shape 2: a rect is outside its box (0 to 10 across, 0 to 6 down). …`), and the bot keeps its hat. A hat sent back as it was read is kept without being checked again. A client draws a drawn hat with the code that draws a kit hat and checks no limit itself; a hat it cannot draw whole is left out. No app has a way to draw or edit a hat.
- **The kit**, in `GET /api/figure`: `kit: { limits: { shapes, commands, stroke: [min, max] }, hat: { box: [w, h], origin: [x, y], pieces } }` and `chief: { outfit: { hat } }`, the Chief of Staff's fixed hat. `pieces` maps a name to `{ means, shapes }`. Draw the hat's box `attach.hat.scale` times as large with its `origin` on `attach.hat.at`, the top of the head: a point `q` of a hat lands at `at + scale × (q − origin)`, and stroke widths grow with it. The hat is in the `head` layer, drawn after the face, so it moves and tilts with the head. A shape is `{ shape: "rect", x, y, w, h, r? }`, `{ shape: "circle", cx, cy, r }`, `{ shape: "line", x1, y1, x2, y2 }` or `{ shape: "path", d: [["M", x, y], ["L", x, y], ["Q", cx, cy, x, y], ["C", c1x, c1y, c2x, c2y, x, y], ["Z"]] }`, with `fill` and/or `stroke` (and `width`), each `"main"` (the bot's colour) or `"deep"` (the colour mixed 45% towards black on a light background, towards white on a dark one). Strokes have round caps and joins. Its shapes are drawn in list order. Draw a hat whole or not at all: one with a shape kind you don't know is left out.
- `GET /api/bots/:id`: one bot.
- `PUT /api/bots/:id` with the fields to change. `pinned` is the bot's place right below the Chief (1 = first; 0 = not pinned). With `pins-shared` in `features` it counts in one order with the pinned groups (see Groups). Send a number to put it there (the other pinned bots, and groups, make room), `true` to pin it last, or `0` to unpin it. `group` is its category, the folder of the list it is in (`""`: none; see Groups for the word). `order` is its place in its category among the bots that aren't pinned (1 = first; 0 while pinned). Send `group` and/or `order` to move it there; a bot moved to another category without an `order` goes last there. Bots never placed show A–Z. A bot has no `learning` and no `friendly`: every bot keeps notes, may propose changes to its main assignment and rules, may write its own skills, and is told to reply in plain language. An older app that sends either, here or in `POST /api/bots`, gets `200` and the field is dropped; a bot stored with either is read without it. Returns the bot as it is kept, its `jobs[]` as every route hands them out (with `when`, `nextRunAt` and `last`); `POST /api/bots` answers the same way. With `needs-text` in `features`, both answers have `waiting` when the Chief or a bot made the call and part of it was held back for the owner's OK in Needs you: a list in the owner's words, one entry per held thing: `"web access"` (WebSearch or WebFetch), `"commands"` (Bash), `"the computer"`, the name of each connected app, `"new main assignment"`, `"new rules"`. The bot in the answer is as it was saved, without them. With nothing held, and for the owner's own changes, `waiting` is absent. `DELETE /api/bots/:id` answers `{ ok, name, computer, skillsRemoved }`: `name` is what the bot was called.
- **Duplicate**, when `features` has `duplicate-bot` (#227). `GET /api/bots/:id/duplicate`: what a copy would get, for the confirmation: `{ name, copies, notCopied, logins, vaultLocked }`. `name` is the suggested name (the original's with the next free number: "Coder" gives "Coder 2", "Coder 2" gives "Coder 3"); `copies` is a list of plain lines to show as they are (its main assignment, its tools, its skills, its connected apps, the computer, who it may message, the Vault entries it may use); `notCopied` is one plain line; `logins` the names of the Vault entries shared with the original; `vaultLocked: true` when Bitwarden is locked, so they can't be read. `POST /api/bots/:id/duplicate` with `{ name? }` makes the copy: a new bot (`201`, as `POST /api/bots` answers it) with a new id, the given name or the suggested one, the same emoji and hat in another colour, and the original's main assignment, rules, description, category, tools, permission mode, model, reasoning, skills, disabled skills, connected apps, computer setting and who it may message. Never its memory, chats, runs, queue, jobs, Board tiles, files or groups. The owner's copy may use every Vault entry the original may (each entry's switches are the entry's own), and the answer has `logins`, their names (one that could not be shared is in `loginsNotShared`, and the copy stays); with Bitwarden locked it is refused (`423`) and nothing is made. A name another bot has is refused (`400`). The Chief's copy (`duplicate_bot`) gets no Vault entry: the answer names them in `loginsNotShared` (or has `vaultLocked: true` when Bitwarden is locked), and what the Chief may not give a bot waits on a card as for `create_bot` (`waiting`).
- **A bot's files**, when `features` has `bot-files` (#235): what the owner (or a teammate) gave the bot, kept in its `uploads/` until the owner deletes them. Every run of the bot is told which there are (the newest 30) and may read them; a file kept in mind (pinned) comes with every task: its text up to 15,000 characters for all of them together, else the summary the bot wrote of it with its own `pin_file`. `GET /api/bots/:id/files` answers `{ files, maxPinned }`, newest first; a file is `{ file, name, size, at, readable, pinned, summary? }`: `file` is its path in the bot's folder (`"uploads/<time>-<random>-<name>"`), `readable` the path of its readable copy or `null`, `summary` the bot's own. The Chief is given `file`, `name`, `size`, `at` and `pinned` only. One file is named by the last part of its `file`: `GET /api/bots/:id/files/<that>` downloads it (the owner only; a PDF opens in the browser, anything else is an attachment); `PUT` with `{ pinned }` keeps it in mind or stops (the owner only; at most `maxPinned`, 10, else `400`); `DELETE` deletes it and its readable copy (the owner, or the Chief's `delete_bot_file`). Both answer the list as `GET` does. An unknown file is `404`. With `document-viewer`, `GET` takes `?view=1` and `?stat=1` (see [The document viewer](#the-document-viewer)).
- `POST /api/bots/:id/run` with `{ prompt?, attachments?, key? }`: send the bot a message, or run its main assignment when there's no `prompt`. Returns the new run (`id`, `status: "running"`, …). `attachments` are what `POST /api/uploads` returned. `key`: see A message sent twice. To a bot that is working it starts beside the runs going, up to three at once ([ADR 062](adr/062-a-message-to-a-busy-bot-starts-beside-the-run-that-is-going.md)); past that the answer is a run that waits, `status: "queued"` (see A message that waits). A `prompt` of the owner's that starts with `/btw` is a quick side question to a working bot (see A quick side question).
- **Running a skill with "/"**, when `features` has `skill-commands` (#237). The owner's message to a bot (`POST /api/bots/:id/run`, from the owner, not the Chief) whose `prompt` starts with `/` and the name of one of the bot's skills, then a space, a line or its end (`/weekly-report for May`), runs that skill: the run's input is Claude Code's invocation of it, `/botdot:weekly-report for May`, with the rest of the message, trimmed, as the skill's arguments. The name is compared without case. The bot's skills are the ones its runs load, while `useSkills` is not `false`: the library skills it has (`skills`), then the Claude account's skills (`GET /api/skills` `machine`) not named in its `disabledSkills` (by name or as `<plugin>:<name>`) and not named like a library skill (#236). Any other message is sent as it is written: a name the bot has no skill of, a `/` not at the very start, a name joined to more text (`/weekly-report/x`). The run's `prompt`, and the chat, keep what the owner wrote. Nothing runs a skill this way in the Chief's chat or in a group. The apps list the skills while the owner types: `/` at the very start of the message box in a bot's own chat offers the bot's skills by name with their `description` from `GET /api/skills` (`library`, then `machine`), filtered by a case-insensitive "contains" on what follows the `/`, at most 6 rows; picking one puts `/<name> ` in place of the `/…` typed; a space after the name, or Escape, closes the list. The rules, for the server and the apps, are in `public/mentions.js` (`suggest`, `pick`, `command`) and the table `test/fixtures/mentions.json` (`cases`, `picks`, `commands`). Without the feature an app offers no list.
- `DELETE /api/bots/:id/queued/:queuedId`: the owner takes a waiting message back (see A message that waits).
- A bot has `overrides`, when `features` has `override`: the rules of its own the owner lifted for it on an `override` card with "Allow always for this bot" (see A rule a bot asks to lift), `[{ id, rule, action, at }]`, at most 10. Only that card adds one: `overrides` in `PUT /api/bots/:id` or `POST /api/bots` is ignored, from the owner, the Chief or a bot. Draw them in the bot's settings as "Rules you lifted", each with its rule, what the bot may do and Revoke. `DELETE /api/bots/:id/overrides/:oid` revokes one, for the owner only (`403` for the Chief, `404` when there is no such one), and answers the bot.
- `POST /api/bots/:id/queued/:queuedId/steer`: the owner steers a waiting message into the run that is going (see Steer).
- `GET /api/runs?botId=:id&limit=50`: its runs, newest first, without its turns in a group (see Groups). The chat is these runs: the owner's `prompt` (and `attachments`), then the bot's `result` or `error`. A reply that ends with a question may have `choices` (2 to 4 short answers, shown as buttons) or `questions` (2 to 6 questions, shown as a card with a box under each; the answers go back as one message, each question followed by a line `→ answer`, `→ –` for one left empty). A Chief message has the same two fields. `&before=:runId` gives the page after that run (older runs, for a chat scrolled up).
- A run that stopped for the owner's OK can have `handedBackAt`: when the botdot server told the Chief to start the bot again on its work (see What follows a yes). Nothing to draw: the server counts that run as carried on from then.
- A run can have `answers`: the id of an earlier run of the bot whose question this message of the owner answers. The server sets it, when the message's words equal one of the `choices` under the newest run of the chat that ended, case and outer spaces aside; a waiting run has it too, and a run that carries on keeps it. No app sends it and there is nothing to draw: the bot is handed that question with the answer. A message from the Chief's `run_bot` or a Board tile never has it.
- A run can have `continues`, when `features` has `ask-continues`: the id of an earlier run of the bot that stopped for the owner's OK, which this run carries on from (see What follows a yes). Draw no message of the owner for it: one that goes on with a request has `customPrompt: false` and that request as its `prompt`, and a job's run that is repeated has the job's task.
- A running run can have `waitingFor: "ok"`, when `features` has `connector-waits`: one of its actions in a connected app waits for the owner's answer (see An action in a connected app). The field is on the run in `GET /api/runs`, `GET /api/runs/:runId` and each of `running` in the feed's `hello`, and it goes when the wait ends. It is not in the stored run.
- `GET /api/runs/:runId`: one run with all its raw `events`. For a run that waits its turn: the waiting run (see A message that waits), with no events.
- **Times.** Each of a run's `events` has `t`: when botdot logged it, in milliseconds since the run started. A run of Claude has `phases`: when it first reached each phase, in milliseconds since botdot started it (at its `startedAt`, when it was taken from the queue): `lookup` (the memory lookup is done), `spawned` (Claude Code is started), `init` (its start-up report), `firstToken` (the first piece the model streams: text, thinking or a tool call), `result` (its result line) and `closed` (the run has ended); a phase it never reached is not there. A Chief message has the same `phases` for its turn, where the lookup is awaited while Claude Code starts. They are for measuring, with nothing to draw. A run from before them has neither `t` nor `phases`.
- `GET /api/runs/:runId/wait?timeout=25`: the same run without events, answered when it has ended or after `timeout` seconds (50 at most), whichever comes first. A run that waits its turn is held for like one that is going: through its start, to its end. With `?seen=chief`, as the Chief's `wait_for_run` calls it, it is also answered at once when the owner writes to the Chief chat whose turn waits, with `ownerWrote: true` on a run that is still going (see The Chief takes a message while it replies).
- `GET /api/runs/:runId/stream?simple`: one run live, as SSE `data:` lines:
  - `{ "type": "start", "run": {…summary} }`
  - `{ "type": "text", "text": "Let me look." }`: what the bot says as it works
  - `{ "type": "step", "tool": "WebSearch", "text": "searched the web for \"game news\"" }`
  - `{ "type": "opening_line", "text": "Checking the weather in Brussels" }`: its opening line (below), also without `?simple`
  - `{ "type": "working_line", "text": "Reading nytimes.com" }`: what its line reads now (see The working line), each time it changes, also without `?simple`
  - `{ "type": "reply_draft", "text": "## Disk\n\nFree space…" }`: its reply as it is written so far (see The reply draft), also without `?simple`
  - `{ "type": "end", "run": {…summary} }`

  It replays what already happened first, then closes after `end`. Without `?simple` you get Claude Code's raw stream.
- `POST /api/runs/:runId/stop`: stop it. `{ ok: true }` when a run was going, `{ ok: false }` when it had ended. With `ended-by` in `features`, the server keeps who called (see Who ended it), and a run that still waits to start (see A message that waits) is taken away: `{ ok: true }`, it never starts and leaves no run, and the feed's `queued` follows when it was a message of the owner, whose key is free again. `404` when there is no such run.

**Who ended it**, when `features` has `ended-by` ([ADR 012](adr/012-a-run-keeps-who-ended-it-and-a-stop-ends-what-its-turn-started.md)). A run that was ended has `endedBy`, in `GET /api/runs`, `GET /api/runs/:id` and the feed's `run_finished`. A run that ended by itself has none, and neither has one a restart cut off (`interrupted`). The caller of a stop route says nothing of who it is: the server knows.

| `endedBy` | Who | `status` | `problem` |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `"owner"` | the owner's Stop, on the run or on the Chief's reply that started it | `stopped` | `You stopped this run before it finished.` |
| `"chief"` | the Chief's `stop_run` | `stopped` | `The Chief stopped this run before it finished.` |
| `"signout"` | a sign-out of Claude, which ends everything that is going | `error` | `This run was stopped because Claude was signed out.` |
| `"idle"` | the silence: a run that writes nothing for 20 minutes is ended ([ADR 043](adr/043-a-run-is-ended-when-it-is-silent-not-when-it-is-long.md)) | `error` | `I stopped because nothing happened for 20 minutes: I seemed stuck. Send it again, or break it into smaller pieces.` |
| `"limit"` | the time limit: a run that goes on for 4 hours is ended, however it works | `error` | `I stopped after 4 hours: this took too long. Send it again, or break it into smaller pieces.` |

**A full disk.** A run that fails because its account's disk is full (Claude, a tool or the bot's computer said "no space left on device", or less than 64 MB of it is free when the run fails) is `error` with `problem` "No space left on the server.", and nothing of a size or a limit. In an account botdot keeps a small spare file in its data, which it removes when a run fails on a full disk, so that the run's record and the next messages can still be written, and makes again after a run that ends well. ([#471](https://github.com/Kordal/bot-dot/issues/471))

- Draw the run's `problem`, as for every run that did not finish. A `stopped` run is not always the owner's doing: read `endedBy`, or the sentence. A server without `ended-by` gives a stopped run the owner's sentence, whoever stopped it, and makes a run that a sign-out ended `stopped`.
- **A run that a sign-out, the silence or the limit ended is a run that failed.** It counts as a new reply and notifies as one (with `job-line`, the scheduled run of a repeating job that a sign-out ended is `quiet`: see A job's line), it is passed on to the Chief's chat that started it (see A late result), it can carry on after the owner's OK (see What follows a yes), and its card of an action in a connected app stays for a late yes. A run the Chief stopped is, for those three, as one the owner stopped.
- **The silence and the limit** are two constants on the server, not settings, and no app shows them ([ADR 043](adr/043-a-run-is-ended-when-it-is-silent-not-when-it-is-long.md)).
