- **A job with a change waiting** is the approved job: `approved: true`, its approved `name`, `kind`, `cron`, `when`, `prompt`, `script`, `notify` and `aiWhen`, and `nextRunAt`, its next run as approved. The scheduler, "Run now" and the Chief's `run_job` run that one. It has three more fields:
  - `change`: the job as proposed, `{ name, kind, when, cron, once, prompt, script, notify, aiWhen, timeoutSeconds, assessment, by, at }`. `when` is the proposed schedule in words, `by` is `"chief"` or `"bot"`, and `at` is when it was proposed, not a one-time moment: `once` is the moment of a job that would run once (what the job itself has as `at`), or `null` for one that would repeat. Draw what differs from the job's own fields as what goes and what comes; a field that is the same did not change. With `change-summary` in `features` it may have `summary`: what the change changes, from what to what, in one sentence of whoever proposed it, as one line of plain text of at most 300 characters. The bot's and the Chief's `update_job` ask for it; a change proposed without one has none. Draw it as text, as what the card says first, and keep what goes and what comes behind it: the sentence is the proposer's own, and the fields are what the yes approves. A job that is added, and a job after its yes, have no `summary`.
  - `no: "Keep as it is"` and `yes: "Approve the change"`: what the two buttons read.
  - It can have `then` (see What follows a yes).
- **What asks** is as before: a change to its kind, script, task, schedule, one-time moment, `aiWhen` or `notify` by a bot, or to a script job by the Chief. A name, a time limit and an assessment sent with such a change wait with it. An edit that changes none of what asks is made at once, as before: on the job, and on a change that waits too, so that the yes does not take back a name given meanwhile. On or off is the job's alone and never waits. A job with `alwaysApprove` changes at once.
- **One change waits per job.** A second one replaces the first; it is laid over the approved job, not over the first change.
- **The owner's own edit** of what the job does is approved at once and drops a waiting change. So does any other edit that is approved at once and changes what the job does, such as the Chief's of an AI job: the change was proposed against a job that is no longer the approved one.
- **The feed** lists it in `approvals.jobs` like a job that waits, with `change: true`, `jobName` as approved, `kind` and `when` as proposed, and `text`, which names the job as approved: `Wants to change a job: <name>, <when>`, or `Wants to change a script: <name>, <when>` when the job as proposed is a script job. The entry has `no` and `yes` as the job has them, and `then` where the job has it. A change that touches a script (the approved job or the proposed one is a script job) has `yesOn: "card"`. The bot's `state` is `needs_you` while a change waits.
- **The answer**, `POST /api/bots/:id/jobs/:jobId/approve`:
  - `{ approve: true }`: the change takes the approved one's place. The answer is the job as changed, without `change`, with `text: "Changed: <when>"`, the new schedule in words, and `carriesOn` when something starts.
  - `{ approve: false }`: the change is dropped and nothing else. The answer is `{ ok: true, kept: true, text: "Kept as it was: <when>" }`, the approved schedule in words. The job is not removed.
  - `{ approve: false }` on an approved job without a change, as a card that is out of date sends it, answers the same and removes nothing. Only a job that was never approved is removed by a no (see Decline).
- **A tile with a change waiting** stays approved: it reads and acts with its approved scripts. In `tiles` and in `pending` of `GET /api/dashboard` it has `change`, the tile as proposed with every field a tile in full has, `scripts` (every script it would run, as strings), `by` and `at`, and the same `no` and `yes`. Its entry of `pending` is in full, with the approved `scripts`: draw the card from it. Its entry of `approvals.tiles` has `change: true`, `by`, `text: "Wants to change “<title>” on your Board (a script)"`, `no`, `yes` and `yesOn: "card"`.
  - `POST /api/dashboard/tiles/:id/approve` with `{ approve: true }` answers `{ ok: true, text: "Changed: <title>" }`, with `carriesOn` when something starts; with `{ approve: false }`, `{ ok: true, kept: true, text: "Kept as it was: <title>" }`. An approved tile is never removed by a no.
  - The yes puts the proposed tile where the tile sits now. A change that touches no script is made at once, as before, on the tile and on a change that waits too: a title, a colour or a size given meanwhile stays after the yes. The owner's own change of a script is approved at once and drops a waiting change.
- **Removed or deleted:** when the job or tile is removed, or its bot deleted, the change goes with it and nothing waits.
- **An app from before `staged-changes`** that finds waiting jobs by `approved: false` shows a job with a change as approved and running, which it is. Its Decline on the feed's entry sends `{ approve: false }` and keeps the job.
- A job or tile that was never approved is as before: `approved: false`, no `change`, `no: "Decline and remove"`, and its no removes it. A server without `staged-changes` writes a change over the approved job, which then waits with `approved: false` and does not run.

**Login requests** (a bot asks for a password, or to pay with a card):
- `GET /api/vault/requests`: the open ones, each with `bot`, `site`, `name`, `username`, `reason`, `kind` and, for logins, `existing` (saved logins for that site, each `{ id, name, username, allowed }`) and `others`: every other saved login a bot can use, saved for another website, each `{ id, name, username, site }`. A card request has no `others`, and with Bitwarden locked it is empty, as `existing` is. No entry in `features`: draw the list of other logins when `others` is there and not empty.
- A login request also has `type`, when `features` has `vault-types`: what is asked for, `login`, `api-token`, `other` or `github-app` (see The Vault's items). The bot or the Chief names it when asking; none or an unknown one is `login`. Set the form's Type from it, and let the owner change it. A card request has none.
- `POST /api/vault/requests/:id` with one of:
  - `{ action: "save", name?, username, password, type?, unlockPassword? }`: save a new login for that site. The password goes straight into the vault or Bitwarden and never to the AI. If Bitwarden is locked, the answer is `423` and asks for `unlockPassword`, the master password. `type` is the type the owner chose. Without it (left out, or `null`) a new item gets the request's `type`, and a login already saved for that site and username keeps its own. The save changes the item already saved for that site and username, and adds one where there is none. Without a username, a save that names a `type` changes only an item of that type: a host can hold a token and a key side by side, and saving one leaves the other as it is. For `other`, send no `username`. Any other `type` is `400` with "Type must be login, api-token, other or github-app": nothing is saved, Bitwarden stays locked, and the request stays open.
  - `{ action: "allow", loginId }`: let the bot use a saved login. With a login of `others`, the request's `site` is also added to that login's additional hosts (`allowedHosts`), so the bot may use it there, in an API call and in its browser, on exactly that host; the audit log gets a `login.host-allowed` line with the login's name, the host and the bot, for every such answer. Ask the owner a second time before sending it, naming the login, its website and the requested host: the password goes to a host the bot asked for. `400` with a sentence to show when the login already has 20 additional hosts (nothing changes and the request stays open); `404` for an unknown `loginId`.
  - `{ action: "approve" }`: allow a card, for this run, card and site, for 15 minutes.
  - `{ action: "decline" }`
- Treat both password fields as secrets: never log them, and clear them after sending.

**Asks** (a permission, a skill from a link, or a tool to install; the `asks` event announces them):
- `GET /api/asks`: the waiting ones. Each has `id`, `kind` (`"permission"`, `"skill"`, `"tool"`, `"bot-change"`, `"connector-action"` or, only for an app that asks for that kind by name, `"vault-switch"` with `?vault-switch=1`, `"vault-remove"` with `?vault-remove=1`, `"vault-file"` with `?vault-file=1`, `"override"` with `?override=1` and `"chief-action"` with `?chief-action=1`), `bot` (`{ id, name, color }`, or null for the Chief), `reason` and `at`, and what its kind adds (`what` for a permission, `skill` and `forBots` for a skill, `title` and `details` for a changed bot and a connector action). With `memory-cards` in `features`, a skill a bot wrote itself that waits is a skill ask with `written: true` (see Memory, The contract). With `connector-waits` in `features`, a connector action also has `rows`, `held` and `stoppedAt`, and `more` when it is too long to show in full (see An action in a connected app). A changed bot also has `by`, and with `ask-continues` in `features` its `title` names what changes and it has `parts`; it can have `yes`, what its yes button reads (see A changed bot). With `needs-text` in `features`, each has `text`, the line its notification says. An ask can have `yesOn: "card"`: its yes is given on its card only (see A tool to install and What a notification says). With `ask-continues` in `features`, an ask can have `then`: what a yes starts (see What follows a yes).
- `POST /api/asks/:id` with `{ yes: true | false }`: the owner's answer. Only the owner; the Chief's tools get `403`. With `ask-continues` in `features`, the answer of a yes can have `carriesOn` (see What follows a yes). For a connector action, `{ yes: true, always: true }` is "Always allow this action" and `{ yes: true }` "Allow once".

**One card per run**, when `features` has `ask-batches` ([#277](https://github.com/Kordal/bot-dot/issues/277), [ADR 056](adr/056-what-one-run-waits-on-is-one-card.md)). Everything one bot run, or the Chief in one chat, waits on is one card in Needs you.
- An ask of `GET /api/asks`, of the `asks` event and of `hello` has `batch` when another ask of the same list shares where it was asked: `{ id, title, count, own? }`. `id` names the card (`run-<run id>` for a bot's run, `chat-<chat id>` for the Chief's chat, where the Chief's own asks and what it proposed for a bot stand); `title` is the card's title in the server's words ("Proton Triage wants 3 things for “Reply to the landlord”", "The Chief wants 2 things in “Trip”"; at most 160 characters); `count` how many asks of that list are on it. An ask alone where it was asked has no `batch`, and is drawn as before. The list counts what it holds: an app that leaves out kinds it doesn't ask for gets the cards of what it is sent.
- `own: true` on an ask that "Allow all" leaves to its own buttons: a switch of a saved login, a secret kept in a file, a Vault entry to remove, and an action in a connected app or of the Chief too long to show in full. Name them on the card ("Allow all leaves “…” to its own Allow.").
- Draw the asks that share a `batch.id` as one card, where the first of them stands: who asked, `title`, **Decline all** and **Allow all** (no Allow all when every ask is `own`), and each ask's own card under them with its own buttons, as without `batch`. With one ask of a batch left (the others answered), draw it as its own card.
- `POST /api/asks/batch/:id` with `{ yes: true | false }`: Allow all or Decline all. Only the owner; the Chief's tools get `403`. Each ask of the card is answered in turn, oldest first, as `POST /api/asks/:id` with `{ yes }` answers it (not `always`): the same grants, the same week-long block after a no, and a run that waits for several asks carries on once, after the last. The answer is `{ ok: true, answers, left }`: `answers`, one entry per ask answered, `{ id, ...the answer of POST /api/asks/:id }` (with `carriesOn` where a yes started something), or `{ id, ok: false, status, error }` for one that could not be answered and keeps its card (show `error`); `left`, the ids of the `own` asks that a yes did not answer. A no answers those too. `404` when no ask is on that card any more.
- **Notifications**: one per card. Ring for the first ask of a card, under its bot's name, with the card's `title` and no answer buttons (a tap opens Needs you); an ask that joins the card later, or the first answered on its own, rings nothing new.
- A server without `ask-batches` sends no `batch`; an app from before it ignores the field and draws each ask on its own card.

**A switch of a saved login** (`kind: "vault-switch"`), when `features` has `vault-switch`. A bot that needs a switch of a login shared with it turned on asks the owner on a card, in its chat and in Needs you, instead of telling them to change it in the Vault. The switches are `insecureTls` ("Accept self-signed certificate") and `inComputer` ("Usable in its computer"), see The Vault's items. Nothing changes before the owner's yes.
  - It is listed only for an app that asks with `GET /api/asks?vault-switch=1`, which draws its card as below: an app from before it would draw it as a skill to install. Its entry in the `asks` event is there for every app, with `text` and `yesOn: "card"`, so a notification offers Decline alone.
  - Besides `id`, `bot`, `reason` (the bot's sentence: why it needs it) and `at`, it has `loginId`, `loginName`, `site`, `vaultSwitch` (`"insecureTls"` or `"inComputer"`) and, as data to draw, `title` ("Let Proxmox Admin accept a self-signed certificate for “Proxmox read-only API token”?", or "Let Proxmox Admin use “Proton Bridge” in its computer?"), `parts` (rows of `{ label, now }`: Login, the login's name and address; Switch, the switch's name as the Vault's form has it; Only for, which logins and bots it reaches), `yes` ("Allow") and `yesOn: "card"`. An `inComputer` one has `warning`: "Proxmox Admin and every other bot on the account's computer will be able to read this secret: they all work there with sudo. Only for a secret that guards something on that computer." Draw it above the buttons, marked as a warning. `text`: "Asks to turn on “Accept self-signed certificate” for “Proxmox read-only API token”".
  - `POST /api/asks/:id` with `{ yes: true }` turns on that one switch of that one login; nothing else of the login changes. The switch is the login's, so every bot the login is shared with has it from then on, as when the owner ticks it in the Vault. The login must still be there and still be shared with the bot: otherwise nothing changes, the card goes, and the answer is `409` with a sentence to show. With Bitwarden locked the answer is `409` and the card stays. Like any ask, the yes can have `carriesOn`: the bot carries on with the request it could not finish. `{ yes: false }` changes nothing, and the bot is not let ask for that switch of that login again for a week.
  - Only the owner answers it: the Chief's tools get `403`, and a bot's run reaches only its own run's tools (`POST /api/vault/session/switch`, under [Not for clients](#not-for-clients)), which add the card and answer nothing.

**Secrets a bot asks to keep in a file** (`kind: "vault-file"`), when `features` has `vault-file` ([#253](https://github.com/Kordal/bot-dot/issues/253), [ADR 051](adr/051-the-owner-may-keep-a-vault-secret-in-a-file.md)). A program that reads a secret from a config or env file (Traefik's DNS challenge, a systemd unit) gets it from botdot: the bot calls `place_secret_file` with the entries (logins and files shared with it), a template, a path, a mode and an owner, and for another server `host` and a saved SSH key. botdot writes the file; the bot never sees a value. The first time for each place (the bot, its entries, the template, the target and its SSH key, the path, the mode and the owner) it asks the owner on a card, and nothing is written before the yes; after it, the same place is written again without a card, e.g. after the secret changed in the Vault.
  - It is listed only for an app that asks with `GET /api/asks?vault-file=1`, which draws its card as a switch's (above): an app from before it would draw it as a skill to install. Its entry in the `asks` event is there for every app, with `text` and `yesOn: "card"`.
  - Besides `id`, `bot`, `reason` and `at`, it has `entries` (`[{ id, name }]`), `target` (`"computer"` or `user@host`), `sshKey`, `path`, `mode`, `owner` and, as data to draw, `title` ("Let Infra Admin keep “OVH app” in a file?"), `parts` (rows of `{ label, now }`: Secret or Secrets, File, Mode, Text (the template, placeholders and all, when there is one), Written by), `warning` (on the account's computer: every bot there can read it, as they all have sudo; on another server: anyone who can read files there), `yes` ("Allow") and `yesOn: "card"`. `text`: "Asks to keep “OVH app” in a file at the account's computer:/etc/traefik/ovh.env". One card per place.
  - `POST /api/asks/:id` with `{ yes: true }` allows that place for that bot, writes nothing itself, and carries the bot on: its next run writes the file. `{ yes: false }` allows nothing, and the bot may not ask for that place again for a week. Every yes, every write and every removal is in the audit log, with the bot and where, never which secret.
  - Only the owner answers it: the Chief's tools get `403`, and a bot's run reaches only `POST /api/vault/session/computer/place-secret-file` (under [Not for clients](#not-for-clients)).

**A Vault entry a bot asks to remove** (`kind: "vault-remove"`), when `features` has `vault-remove` ([#132](https://github.com/Kordal/bot-dot/issues/132)). A bot can ask to remove any entry its `list_logins` shows, shared with it or not (every entry is listed to every bot), with its vault tool `remove_login` (`{ entry, reason }`: the entry's name, or a login's address). It asks the owner on a card, in its chat and in Needs you; nothing is removed before the owner's yes, and no secret of the entry is read for it. Only an owner's Vault in Bitwarden: with botdot's built-in vault, which has no Trash, the tool is refused.
  - It is listed only for an app that asks with `GET /api/asks?vault-remove=1`, which draws its card as a switch's (above): an app from before it would draw it as a skill to install. Its entry in the `asks` event is there for every app, with `text` and `yesOn: "card"`, so a notification offers Decline alone.
  - Besides `id`, `bot`, `reason` (the bot's sentence: why it is no longer needed) and `at`, it has `itemId`, `loginName` (the entry's name), `site` (a login's address, else empty) and, as data to draw, `title` ("Let VPS Admin remove “Uptime Kuma netcup API key” from the Vault?"), `parts` (rows of `{ label, now }`: Entry, its name and address; Asked by, the bot's name; Goes to, "Bitwarden's Trash, where you can restore it for 30 days"), `yes` ("Allow") and `yesOn: "card"`. `text`: "Asks to remove “Uptime Kuma netcup API key” from the Vault". One card per bot and entry.
  - `POST /api/asks/:id` with `{ yes: true }` moves the entry to Bitwarden's Trash (`bw delete item`, never `--permanent`), in the one queue every `bw` command of botdot waits in, as the Vault page's delete does; the owner can restore it from the Trash in Bitwarden for 30 days, then shared with no bot. Another bot's card for the same entry goes with it. The entry must still be in the Vault: otherwise nothing changes, the card goes, and the answer is `409` with a sentence to show. With Bitwarden locked the answer is `409` and the card stays. When Bitwarden does not answer within 45 seconds (`BOT_DOT_VAULT_WAIT_MS`), the answer is `503` ("Bitwarden didn't answer in time. It goes to the Trash once it answers."): the removal goes on, and the card goes once it is done; a yes meanwhile answers `409` and removes nothing twice. Like any ask, the yes can have `carriesOn`: the bot carries on with its request, told "The owner allowed you to: remove “<entry>” from the Vault (it is in Bitwarden's Trash now)." `{ yes: false }` leaves the entry, and the bot is not let ask to remove that entry again for a week.
  - Only the owner answers it: the Chief's tools get `403`, and a bot's run reaches only its own run's tools (`POST /api/vault/session/remove`, under [Not for clients](#not-for-clients)), which add the card and answer `{ ok: true, name }`, or `{ ok: false, error }` for an entry that is not in the Vault, a name that several entries have (name it by its address then), a locked Bitwarden, a built-in vault, and an entry the owner said no to within the week.
  - **The Chief asks for it too**, when `features` has `chief-vault-remove` (#183): its tools send `POST /api/vault/remove` with `{ entry, reason }` (`remove_vault_item`), for any entry the Vault lists, as for a bot. The card is the same kind with `botId: null` and `bot: null`, `chatId` (the Chief chat it asked in), the title "Let the Chief remove “…” from the Vault?" and Asked by "the Chief"; one card per entry for the Chief. It stands in Needs you and in the Chief's chats, as a skill the Chief asks for does. Its answer is `201 { ok: true, id, name }`. It is refused `403` in a turn the owner did not start, `400` without an entry, for one the Vault does not list or that several entries are called, for a built-in vault and for an entry the owner said no to the Chief within the week, and `423` while Bitwarden is locked, each with the sentence the Chief passes on; no card is added then. The yes moves the entry to the Trash as above and tells the Chief in its chat (What follows a yes); a no writes "Declined: removing “…” from the Vault." there. Draw it as a bot's card.

**A rule a bot asks to lift** (`kind: "override"`), when `features` has `override` ([#253](https://github.com/Kordal/bot-dot/issues/253), [ADR 052](adr/052-the-owner-may-lift-a-bot-rule-on-a-card.md)). When one of a bot's own rules is what stops what the owner asked, the bot asks with its tool `ask_override` (`{ rule, action, reason }`: the rule in its words, the one action it would take, and why) instead of refusing. One card per bot, rule and action.
  - It is listed only for an app that asks with `GET /api/asks?override=1`: an app from before it would draw it as a skill to install. Its entry in the `asks` event is there for every app, with `text` and `yesOn: "card"`, so a notification offers Decline alone.
  - Besides `id`, `bot`, `reason` and `at`, it has `rule`, `action` and, as data to draw: `title` ("Let <bot> do this, though its rules say no?"), `parts` (`Its rule` and `What it would do`), `warning`, `yes` ("Allow once") and `always` ("Allow always for this bot"). Draw three buttons: Decline, `always` and `yes`.
  - `POST /api/asks/:id` with `{ yes: true }` lets the waiting run carry on with "The owner allowed you to: <action>, once, in this run, though your rule “<rule>” says otherwise." When no run can carry on (it was stopped, or 24 hours passed), the bot's next run is told it, once, and no later one. With `{ yes: true, always: true }` it also keeps the grant in the bot's `overrides` (above) and every later run of the bot is told it, in botdot's words, at the start of its task; `409` when the bot has 10 already. A no blocks the same ask for a week.
  - Only the owner answers it: the Chief's tools and a bot's run get `403`. The bot's tool is `POST /api/self/session/ask` with `kind: "override"` (under [Not for clients](#not-for-clients)), refused with words for an empty rule or action, a rule lifted for that action already, and a no within the week. The audit log records `override.allowed` (once or always) and `override.revoked` with the bot and the grant's id, never its words. `rule` and `action` are kept on one line, with `<` and `>` written `‹` and `›`.
  - It lifts the bot's own rules only. These stay whatever a card says: a secret value in a chat or in the model's context, a bot answering its own card, and another account's data.

**An action in a connected app** (`kind: "connector-action"`), when `features` has `connector-waits` ([ADR 010](adr/010-a-connected-app-action-waits-in-its-run.md)). A bot that wants to send, post, change or delete through a connected app needs the owner's OK. Its run waits for the answer, inside the run, for ten minutes: nothing is done before the answer, and the bot does not give up meanwhile.
- These fields are on the ask in `GET /api/asks` and on its entry of the `asks` event and of `hello`.
- `title`: `<Action> with <App>?`, the action in words from its name, as `text` has them: "Send email with Gmail?".
- `rows`: one `{ label, value }` per argument of the action, in the order the bot gave them. Draw them as rows, as the `facts` of a tool.
  - `label` is the argument's name in words (`is_html` reads "Is html"), with every letter and digit of the name in any script (`Imię` reads "Imię", `日本語` reads "日本語") and, in `GET /api/asks`, never clipped, without the characters that draw nothing, "Value" for a name with no letter and no digit (accents alone are none), and "To", "Subject" and "Text" for the usual names of a recipient (`to`, `recipient`, `recipients`, `recipient_email`), a subject (`subject`) and a body (`body`, `text`, `message`). One card has each label once: of several arguments that would read the same, the first keeps the label and the others read by their own names ("Text", then "Message"), with a number where that is taken too ("Text (2)").
  - `value` is text: a list joined with commas, yes or no, a nested value as "Name: value" pairs, a file as "the file <name>".
  - **In `GET /api/asks` the rows are whole:** a row for every argument and every value in full. The owner approves what the card shows, and the action runs with the arguments as the bot sent them: an opened card shows every row and every value in full; a closed one may show the start of each, as other cards do.
  - **An action too long to show in full has `more: true` on the ask, beside `rows`:** one of more than 200 arguments, or whose rows hold more than 100,000 characters together, labels and values. Its `rows` are then the first rows that fit both limits, each whole: no value is cut in the middle, no row has a `more` of its own, and there can be none at all (one value longer than the limit). Draw the rows, one line "Too long to show in full.", Decline and "Always allow this action", and no "Allow once": it would send what the card does not show, and the server refuses it (see the answer, below). Do not draw `details` for it: they are as long as the action. An ask without `more` is whole. The run waits for the answer as for any other action, and the bot is told nothing else.
  - **In the `asks` event and in `hello` they are the short form:** a label of at most 60 characters and a value of at most 500, each clipped with "…", and at most 30 rows, followed by one row "More" that says how many were left out. Each cut is marked: `more: true` on a row whose label or value was clipped, and `more: true` on the entry, beside `rows`, when rows were left out. An entry without either is whole. A clipped label is not told apart from another: two long names that start alike read the same here, each on a row with `more`. A card drawn from these rows with a `more` is not all a yes approves: show `details` under them where you have it, and offer no yes ("Allow once", "Always allow this action") until `GET /api/asks` has brought the ask: its rows whole, or with the `more` of an action too long to show in full. `more` beside the rows says one thing here (rows were left out of the short form) and another on an ask of the list (the action is too long, above): an app tells them apart by where it read the ask. A notification has no yes for this ask either (`yesOn: "card"`).
- `held`: `true` while its run waits for the answer. Then a yes lets that same run go on, and the ask has no `then`. Otherwise `false`, with `stoppedAt`: when the wait ended. That is ten minutes after the ask was made, or when its run ended, or when the botdot server started, since no run waits across a restart.
- `then`, on an ask that is not held: `{ who: "bot", text: "It carries on when you allow it." }`, whoever started the run. A yes starts the bot again (see What follows a yes), and its answer has `carriesOn`: `it carries on`, or `it carries on when it is free`. An ask of `GET /api/asks` that is not held always has it: one whose yes could start nothing any more goes as the list is read. Its entry of the `asks` event and of `hello` can be without `then` for up to a minute before the ask goes.
- `details` stays, for an app from before `rows`: the rule of the two answers and the arguments as they were sent. Draw `rows` when they are there.
- **The run** has `waitingFor: "ok"` while one of its actions waits (see Bots), the feed says `run_waits` when that begins and when it ends, and the bot's `state` is `needs_you` for as long as the ask is there.
- **The card stands in the bot's chat too**, at its end, under the line that says "Waiting for your OK": the same card as in Needs you, drawn from the same ask of `GET /api/asks` (its `botId`, outside a group), with the same rows and buttons, and answered with the same `POST /api/asks/:id`. The card of the run that waits is the ask with `held: true` of that bot (with runs going side by side, ADR 062, each run that waits has its own). Draw it again whenever the list is read, since the list arrives after the chat is drawn. Once answered, in the chat or in Needs you, the chat shows the card's title and the answer as one line until the owner leaves it. Nothing new is sent for this: an app of any version draws it from what it has.
- **The answer.**
  - "Allow once" (`{ yes: true }`) counts once: for one run, this action and these exact arguments. While the run waits, it is that run, and its call goes through. On an ask that is not held it is the run this yes starts: the bot is handed the action and the stored arguments, and that run's call of this action with exactly these arguments goes through once, without a new card. A second call, or other arguments, makes a new ask and a new wait. The grant is gone when that run ends, used or not.
  - "Always allow this action" (`{ yes: true, always: true }`) saves the lasting grant, for this bot and this action with any arguments (`GET` and `DELETE /api/bots/:id/connector-actions`), and carries on the same way.
  - A no (`{ yes: false }`): nothing is done. A run that waits is told that the owner declined, and goes on without the action.
  - `409` with a sentence to show, "This is too long to show in full, so it can't be allowed once. Decline it, or always allow this action.", for a yes without `always: true` on an ask with `more`, whether its run still waits or not: nothing is granted, nothing starts and the ask stays. `{ yes: true, always: true }` and a no work on it as on any other.
  - `409` with a sentence to show, "<Bot> no longer waits for this, so nothing was done. Ask it again.", for "Allow once" on an ask whose yes can start nothing any more; the ask is gone. "Always allow this action" on such an ask saves the lasting grant and answers `{ ok: true }` without `carriesOn`: nothing starts. `429` with a sentence to show when the bot already has ten tasks waiting: nothing is granted and the ask stays.
- **The ask goes** without an answer when the owner or the Chief stops the run that waits (`status: "stopped"`). The ask of a run that a sign-out of Claude or the time limit ended stays, as after any run that failed (see Who ended it). One that is not held goes when its yes could start nothing: when the owner sends that bot a new message, 24 hours after it was made, when the bot is deleted, when its run was a job's and the bot is paused (all its jobs switched off), when its run already carried on or the Chief was told to start the bot again on it (`handedBackAt`). What the owner writes in the Chief's chat does not take it away, also where the Chief started that run: this yes starts the bot itself. The `asks` event says so.
- A server without `connector-waits` answers the bot at once, so no run waits; its ask has `title` and `details` only, and "Allow once" after the run ended answers `400` with "That run has ended. Start a new run and request approval again."

**A changed bot** (`kind: "bot-change"`): what the Chief, or the bot itself, wants to change about a bot and may not without the owner.
- A `permission` ask's `permission` is `"web"` (WebSearch and WebFetch), `"files"` (Edit and Write), `"commands"` (Bash) or `"helpers"` (Agent and Task, #226: Claude Code's helpers, which work in parallel inside the bot's one run with its tools and end with it). Its yes adds those tools to the bot's `allowedTools`. An app shows `what` and `text` as they come, so an older app draws a `"helpers"` card all the same. A refused tool asks nothing by itself ([ADR 054](adr/054-a-permission-card-comes-only-from-the-bots-ask.md)): the card comes only from the bot's `ask_permission`, during the run.
- A bot asks for a setting that is switched off for it with `ask_permission` (#137): `"message"` with `bots` (ids or names) to message bots it may not message yet, or `"computer"` for a seat on the account's computer. That is a changed bot proposed by the bot (`by: "bot"`) with `asked` (`"message"` or `"computer"`) and, for `"message"`, `bots` (`[{ id, name }]`): its `title` is `Let <bot> message <names>?` or `Let <bot> have a computer?`, its `yes` is `"Allow"`, its `text` "Asks to message <names>" or "Asks to have a computer", and its one part "May message" with the names, or "Computer". Its yes adds those bots to `mayMessage`, or sets `computer: "linux"`, as the bot's settings do, and carries on the run that asked (What follows a yes); a computer still waits for a free one under the account's share. Bots asked for while its messaging card waits join that card. After a no, the same ask (that bot for messaging) is refused for 7 days. Nothing the account's admin limits can be asked for.
- These fields are on the ask in `GET /api/asks` and, with `ask-continues` in `features`, on its entry of the `asks` event and of `hello`.
- `by`: `"chief"` or `"bot"`, who proposed it. `chatId`: the Chief chat it was asked for in, when the Chief asked from one. An app can draw the card in that chat too; one answer settles it everywhere.
- `title`, in the owner's words. A setting the bot asked for: see above. One permission and nothing else: `Let <bot> <label>?`, with the label of that permission ("browse the web", "run commands"). Anything else: `Change <bot>: <list>?`, the list being what `waiting` says of the same change (see What waits after the Chief's change), joined with commas and "and": "Change Mail Sorter: commands and new rules?". A server without `ask-continues` sends "Approve changes to this bot?".
- `parts`: one entry per changed field, `{ label, was?, now }`. A text has both, each in full: `label` "Main assignment" or "Rules", `was` the text as it is (empty when there is none) and `now` the proposed one. Draw the old text struck and the new under it. Anything else has `label` and `now` only, to draw in one line: "What it may do" with the permissions it gets ("browse the web and run commands"), "Computer" ("a screen on the account's computer, which every bot there shares with sudo, on your home network: a saved login used there, they can all read"), "May message" with the names of the bots it may message then, "Connected apps" with the names of the apps it gets. No `title` and no `parts` holds a field's own name, a tool's name or an app's slug.
- `yes`: what the card's yes button reads, on a change whose parts only give permissions: `"Allow"`. Any other change has none: the button reads "Approve". Draw the word as text, without the blanks around it. No entry in `features`: draw it when it is there.
- `details` stays, for an app from before `parts`: the fields as text. Draw `parts` when they are there.
- One card per grant: while a changed bot that gives a permission waits, a run of that bot that is refused it, or asks for it, makes no `permission` ask and waits for this card; and a changed bot takes the place of a `permission` ask that waits for the same permission, whose card goes. A yes on the one card then carries those runs on as it would have on theirs, and no longer meets "The bot changed since this proposal" because the other card was answered first. A change counts as giving a permission when it adds any tool of it, also by a narrower rule.
- A yes answers `409` with "The bot changed since this proposal. Decline it and ask for a fresh proposal." when a field it changes is no longer as it was when it was proposed (the owner edited it, or allowed another permission on its own card): nothing is applied and the card stays. Such a card has no `then`, and a run that is refused the permission meanwhile gets a `permission` ask of its own.

**What follows a yes**, when `features` has `ask-continues` ([ADR 009](adr/009-the-botdot-server-carries-on-after-an-ok.md)). A permission or a skill that a bot's run asked for keeps that run, and what the Chief asked for in a chat keeps that chat. After the owner's yes the botdot server itself carries on with the work that stopped, once: nobody sends the request again. The server decides it, wherever the yes is given, so an app from before this feature carries on all the same. An app only shows what the server says.
- **`then`** on an ask (`GET /api/asks`, the `asks` event, `hello`): present when a yes would start something, absent when it only changes the setting. Show `text` as one line on the card, as it is.
  - `who`: `"bot"` or `"chief"`.
  - `text`, one sentence:
    - a request of the owner (their message, or their tap on a tile of the Board): `It carries on with “<request>” when you allow it.`, the request as the owner wrote it, in one line, at most 80 characters, clipped with "…";
    - a job's run, at its time or from "Run now": `It runs “<job name>” again when you allow it.`;
    - a teammate's request: `It carries on with <teammate's name>'s request when you allow it.`;
    - a run the Chief started: `who` is `"chief"` and `text` is `The Chief carries on in “<chat title>” when you allow it.`, with `chatId`, that Chief chat, and `here`: `The Chief carries on in this chat when you allow it.`, the sentence for a card drawn in that chat;
    - what the Chief asked for in a chat (see What the Chief asked for): the same `who`, `text`, `chatId` and `here`, for the chat in which the Chief will be told. It comes before the sentence of a run that waits for the same card.
  - It is worked out when the ask is read, so it goes by itself, and the yes then only changes the setting: when the owner sent that bot a message after that run asked (a later request that was refused the same thing waits for the same card and carries on in its place; one the bot answered is on no card, and nothing that asked before it carries on; a message sent while that run was still going, before it asked, does not count, and a run that is refused after the message counts for itself), 24 hours after the ask was made, when the bot is deleted, when the owner or the Chief stopped that run (not when a sign-out of Claude or the time limit ended it: that run failed, see Who ended it), when the run is no longer found or was carried on from already, when a job ran again since, was removed, waits for approval or was switched off, or its bot is paused (all its jobs switched off: that stops a job's run from being repeated, not what the owner, the Chief or a teammate asked of the bot), and, for a run the Chief started, when the Chief chat that started it is gone, when the owner wrote in that chat after that run asked (not for an action in a connected app, whose yes starts the bot itself), or when the Chief was already told to start the bot again on it (`handedBackAt` on the run). The `asks` event carries `then` as it was when the event was sent; nothing is sent when it goes. Read `GET /api/asks` for how it stands.
  - A second run that is refused the same thing joins the waiting ask: one card, an `asks` event, and `then` speaks of the run that asked last among those that can carry on. Each of them carries on after the yes, except that a run the owner sent the bot a message after does not, so of the owner's own requests on one card the latest carries on, and that of the runs the Chief started from one chat only the latest counts: one yes gives the Chief one turn in a chat.
  - A run that waits for several asks carries on once, when the last of them is answered and at least one answer was a yes: each of its cards has `then`, and only the last answer starts it. That last answer can be a no.
- **`carriesOn`** in the answer of a yes (`POST /api/asks/:id`, and the approve routes of a job and a tile): `{ who, text }` when that yes started something or put it in the bot's queue, absent otherwise (a yes that only changed the setting, a run that still waits for another answer, a bot with too much waiting). `text` is `it carries on`, `it carries on when it is free` (the bot is working: the work waits its turn, also across a restart of the botdot server) or `the Chief carries on`. Print it after the app's own word for the answer: "Allowed · it carries on". When a yes starts the Chief's own turn and a bot's run, it names the Chief. The answer of a no is as before.
- **What carries on**, by who started the run that stopped:
  - the owner: a new run of the bot with `continues`, the id of the run that stopped. The bot is handed the request word for word with its files, its earlier reply, and what was allowed and declined. The run's `prompt` is that request and its `customPrompt` is `false`: draw no message of the owner for a run with `continues`, the request shows once, above the first reply. Its reply is a new reply like any answer to the owner;
  - a job: the job runs once more with its own task, as a run with `continues`, under the job's `notify`. It is drawn as any run of that job;
  - a teammate: a new run with `continues`, `trigger: "bot"` and the teammate as `fromBot`. Its final reply goes to the teammate as a message from the bot (`sentTo`), unless the run sent one itself;
  - the Chief: no run. The Chief gets a turn in the chat that started the run (`chief_reply`) and starts the bot again itself. When that turn brings no reply, botdot writes one `notice` message there: "News Watcher may now browse the web." The run that stopped gets `handedBackAt` and counts as carried on from then: it is in no ask's `then`, and an action in a connected app that it stopped waiting for goes (its yes would start the bot a second time). When the owner wrote in that chat after that run asked (was refused, or asked through its own tools), they took the work up there: no turn, the setting still changes, and botdot writes one `notice` message in that chat: "Approved: web access for News Watcher." Each run counts from the moment it asked itself: the message that led the Chief to start the run, and anything the owner wrote there while the run was still going, does not count, also when that run joined a card that is older than the message. When botdot already starts the bot from that run itself (a late yes on an action in a connected app, whose work waits behind a busy bot), the Chief gets no turn either: that work is told of this answer too, also when the owner wrote in that chat since, and `carriesOn` says `it carries on when it is free`.
- The `trigger`, `startedBy`, `fromBot`, `jobId` and `chainId` of a run with `continues` are those of the run that stopped, so it counts as new, is folded and notifies as that run did.
- **What the Chief asked for** in a chat: a changed bot, a skill (`POST /api/asks` by the Chief's tools), a script job and a Board tile that wait for the owner. Each keeps the chat its tools were called from, and the Chief is told there of the answer.
  - `then`, as above, is on the ask of a changed bot and of a skill, on a waiting job (its entry of `approvals.jobs`, and the job itself wherever a bot's `jobs` are sent) and on a waiting tile (its entry of `approvals.tiles` and of `pending` in `GET /api/dashboard`). A waiting job and a waiting tile also have `chatId` and `askedAt`, where and when the Chief asked; an approved one has neither.
  - A yes gives the Chief a turn in that chat (`chief_started`, then `chief_reply`), and the answer has `carriesOn: { who: "chief", text: "the Chief carries on" }`: the answer of `POST /api/asks/:id` for a changed bot and a skill, of `POST /api/bots/:id/jobs/:jobId/approve` for a job (beside the job; "Always approve" is a yes), and of `POST /api/dashboard/tiles/:id/approve` for a tile (`{ ok: true, carriesOn }`). While the Chief replies in that chat the turn waits for that reply, also across a restart of the botdot server. When the turn brings no reply, botdot writes one `notice` message of what is now true: "News Watcher can browse the web now."
  - No turn, no `then` and no `carriesOn` when the owner wrote in that chat after the Chief asked, or 24 hours after it asked. The setting still changes, and botdot writes one `notice` message in that chat: "Approved: web access for News Watcher."
  - A no starts nothing, and botdot writes "Declined: web access for News Watcher." there. The Chief reads botdot's lines with its next turn in that chat.
  - With that chat deleted, the Chief is told in the chat the owner used last, which `then` names, or in a new one, and `then` then has `who` and `text` only: `The Chief carries on in a new chat when you allow it.` No line is written for a chat that is gone.
  - A run of the bot that waits for the same card carries on as well, by its own rules. One that the Chief itself started is started again by that one turn.
  - A change to an approved job or tile that the Chief asked for in a chat (see A change that waits) is told there too, the yes and the no: "Approved: the change to the script “Disk check” of Disk Keeper.", "Declined: the change to the tile “Door” on your Board."
  - What was asked for from no chat (by a tools server from before this) has none of this: its yes changes the setting, as before.
- **What a bot proposed** in a run, when `features` has `proposal-carries-on`: a job it added or changed, a tile it suggested for the Board, and a change to its own instructions. Each keeps the run it was proposed in, and after the owner's yes the bot carries on with the request of that run.
  - `then`, as above, is on the waiting job (the job itself and its entry of `approvals.jobs`; a change's `then` is on the job beside `change`), on the suggestion (each of `suggestions` in `GET /api/dashboard`, beside `id`, `bot`, `reason` and `tile`) and on the ask of the changed bot.
  - The answer of the yes has `carriesOn`, `{ who: "bot", text }` as above: `POST /api/bots/:id/jobs/:jobId/approve` (beside the job), `POST /api/dashboard/suggestions/:id` with `{ add: true }` (`{ ok: true, tile, carriesOn }`) and `POST /api/asks/:id`.
  - The bot gets one new run with `continues`, handed the request word for word, its earlier reply and what was approved: "add the job “Monday plan”", "change the job “Morning report”", "put the tile “This week” on their Board", "change your main assignment".
  - Only a request of the owner or of a teammate carries on, by the rules above: not once the owner sent that bot a message after the proposal, not 24 hours after it, and once for a run that waits for several answers. A proposal made in a job's own run, or in a run the Chief started, has no `then`, and its yes changes the setting only. A no starts nothing.
  - The run stays on the server: no route sends it.
- A server without `ask-continues` sends none of these fields, and its yes changes the setting only.

**What a notification says**, when `features` has `needs-text`. Everything that waits for the owner has `text`: one line that can stand under the bot's name, worded by the server. Show it as it is. It never holds a script or an argument of an action, and never more than 160 characters; a name that would make it longer is clipped with "…".
- An ask (`GET /api/asks`, the `asks` event, `hello`), by `kind`:
  - `permission`: "Asks to browse the web", "Asks to change files", "Asks to run commands" or "Asks to use helpers";
  - `skill`: "Asks to install the skill “<name>”";
  - `tool`: "Asks to install gh", from a link "Asks to install stripe from a link";
  - `bot-change`: a setting the bot asked for, "Asks to message <names>" or "Asks to have a computer"; otherwise "The Chief wants to change <what>" or, proposed by the bot itself, "Wants to change <what>". <what> names each changed field, joined with commas and "and": "its main assignment", "its rules", "what it may do" (its tools), "its computer", "its connected apps", "its computer's network";
  - `connector-action`: "Wants to send email with Gmail": the action in lowercase words from its name, and the app;
  - `vault-switch`: "Asks to turn on “Accept self-signed certificate” for “<login>”", or "“Usable in its computer”".
  - `vault-remove`: "Asks to remove “<entry>” from the Vault".
  - `vault-file`: "Asks to keep “<entry>” in a file at <where>".
  - `override`: "Asks to lift a rule: <what it would do>".
- A waiting job (`approvals.jobs`) also has `kind` (`"script"` or `"ai"`) and `when`, its schedule in the words a job's `when` has: "Wants to add a script: <name>, <when>", or "Wants to add a job: <name>, <when>" for an AI job. A job that waits after a change reads the same.
- A waiting tile (`approvals.tiles`): "Wants to put “<title>” on your Board (a script)".
- `yesOn: "card"` (see A tool to install) is sent for a tool from a link, a changed bot, a connected-app action, a switch of a saved login, a Vault entry a bot asks to remove, a rule a bot asks to lift, a skill a bot wrote (`written: true`), a script job and a tile: what they would do shows on their card and not in one line, so their notification offers Decline alone and a tap opens the card. A permission, a skill from a link, a tool from the registry and an AI job have none and are answered from the notification as before.
- A server without `needs-text` sends `text` for a `tool` ask only, and `yesOn` for a tool from a link only.

**A tool to install**, when `features` has `tools` ([ADR 007](adr/007-installed-tools-are-pinned-lists-placed-by-a-tools-user.md)). The Chief asks for a command-line tool to be installed on the botdot server for one bot; nothing is installed before the owner's Install. A `tool` ask carries everything its card shows, as data: draw it, and build no text of your own.
- `title`: "Install gh for Coder?".
- `meta`: who asked, "asked by the Chief" or "Coder asked the Chief". For a tool from a link, whose link it is comes first: "from a link you gave · asked by the Chief", or "from a link the Chief proposed · asked by the Chief".
- `body`: what the tool is and why, as plain text with a blank line between: "Github CLI.\n\nWhy: Coder needs it to open the pull request." For a tool from a link the first part is the Chief's few words and botdot's own sentence: "Stripe CLI, as described on its page. botdot has not checked what this program does."
- `warning`: absent for a tool from the registry. A tool from a link has one, as plain text: "Not from the approved list. Make sure you trust the source." It may take two lines of a card on a phone. Show all of it above the facts, marked as a warning, also on a card that shows only the start of its body.
- `facts`: rows of `{ label, value }`, in order. For a registry tool: Version, Source, Size (what is downloaded), Runs as, Stays. For a tool from a link: Version (only when the Chief gave one), Source (the link's host and path), File (the file's name), Checksum ("sha256 3f9a…c2e1", the first and last four characters), Size, Runs as. Draw every row you get, also labels you don't know.
- `tool`: `{ name, version, summary, source, platform, size, packages }`, for a client that needs the parts. `source` is `{ type: "registry", registry: "conda-forge" }`, or for a tool from a link `{ type: "link", url, sha256, command, from }`: the address, the whole sha256 of the file botdot downloaded when the Chief asked, the command's name (the tool's `name`), and `from`, `"owner"` when that address stands in one of the owner's own messages in the Chief chat that asked and `"chief"` otherwise. A tool from a link has no `packages`, and its `version` is `""` when the Chief gave none. `askedBy` is `"chief"` or `{ bot, runId }` when a bot asked the Chief.
- **A tool from a link** is one file over https: a program for the botdot server's processor, or a `.tar.gz` or `.zip` that holds the named command as a regular file. When the Chief asks, botdot downloads it once to note its sha256 and size; on Install it downloads it again and installs it only when the sha256 is the same, and the same holds whenever botdot has to install it again (after a restore). Nothing from the file is run by the ask or the install. botdot does not check what the program does, who made it, or that the Chief's version and description are true.
- Answer it like any ask. `{ yes: true }` returns `{ ok: true, installing: true }` at once and the card is gone; the install goes on by itself, and its result (installed, or what went wrong) arrives as a Chief reply in the chat that asked (`chief_reply`). When the Chief's turn about it cannot start or ends with an error (Claude is not signed in), botdot writes the result in that chat itself, as one `notice` message: "gh 2.101.0 is installed for Coder." or "⚠️ gh 2.101.0 was not installed for Coder: The registry could not be reached." `{ yes: false }` installs nothing, and the same tool is not asked for that bot again for a week: not from the registry and not from a link, also a link the owner gives after that no. The Chief's ask gets `400` with that reason, and no card is added.
- The ask's notification is the bot's name with the `text` of the `asks` event: "Asks to install gh", or for a tool from a link "Asks to install stripe from a link". Show the line as it is; the wording is the server's.
- `yesOn`: where the owner's yes is given. `"card"` means on the ask's card and nowhere else: its notification offers Decline and no yes button, and a tap on it opens Needs you. A tool from a link has it, so that it is approved where its warning, whose link it is, the address and the checksum show; a tool from the registry does not, and keeps Install on its notification. With `needs-text` in `features`, a changed bot, a connected-app action, a skill a bot wrote, a waiting script job and a waiting tile have it too (see What a notification says). The field is on the ask (`GET /api/asks`) and on its entry in the `asks` event and in `hello`, and it is not a tool's alone: honour it on an ask of any kind. Read the value without the blanks around it. An ask without it, or with a value you don't know, is answered from its notification as before. The server takes the same `POST /api/asks/:id` from a card and from a notification, so an app from before this field still shows Install there.
- An app from before `tools` shows this ask as a skill card with an empty name, and its answer still works.
- There is no page for installed tools: the owner asks the Chief what a bot has, and to remove one. These are the Chief's tools' own, not for clients: `POST /api/asks` with `{ kind: "tool", bot, name, reason, chatId?, run? }` (the ask: `201` with the ask as above, or `400` with the reason no card was added; `run` is the run in which that bot asked the Chief), with `{ kind: "tool", bot, link, command, version?, what?, reason, chatId?, run? }` for a tool from a link (`400` with the reason when the link is not https or redirects away from it, does not answer, is over 200 MB, is a script, a `.deb` or an `.rpm`, is for another processor, or is an archive without the named command, with a link or a special file in it, or with a path that leaves its folder) or `{ kind: "skill", url, bots, reason }`, `GET /api/tools` (`?bot=` for one bot: `{ available, tools: [{ bot, name, version, summary, commands, source, askedBy, approvedAt, missing?, problem? }] }`; `missing: true` with `problem`, the reason, is a tool on the bot's list that could not be installed again at a start: the bot is without it, and it can be asked for again like a tool the bot never had), `GET /api/tools/find?name=` (`{ registry, exact, matches: [{ name, summary, latest, forThisServer }], server }`; `server` is the system and processor a release file from a link must be for, "Linux, arm64 (aarch64)") and `DELETE /api/tools/:botId/:name`.

**Bitwarden:**
- `GET /api/vault/bitwarden`: `{ installed, configured, unlocked, email, items, wantedBy, … }`.
- `POST /api/vault/bitwarden/unlock` with `{ password }`: the master password. It's kept only in memory.
- `POST /api/vault/bitwarden/lock`.

**Shared-memory changes waiting for an OK** (see [Memory](#memory) for the fields):
- `GET /api/memory/pending`: the list.
- `POST /api/memory/pending/:id` with `{ approve: true | false }`.

## Skills from a link

Only the web page imports skills: the Android app has no import, so no `features` entry announces the routes.

- `POST /api/skills/import/preview` with `{ url }`: a skills.sh link, or a GitHub link to a skill's folder, its `SKILL.md`, or a repository or folder. GitHub is read with one listing of the folder. A link to one skill answers `{ id, name, exists, description, allowedTools, instructions, files: [{ path, size }], folder, totalBytes, fileCount, scripts, url }`. A link to a repository or folder that holds several skills (#30) answers `{ id, many: true, url, folder, skills: [{ folder, name, description, exists, fileCount, scripts, allowedTools, totalBytes, problem? }] }`, one entry per folder with a `SKILL.md`, by folder; a `SKILL.md` inside another skill's folder is a file of that skill. `name` is the name it is imported under, `exists` that the library has a skill of that name, and `problem` a sentence why it cannot be imported (no usable name, over the limits of one skill, a name another skill of the list has). Only each skill's `SKILL.md` is read for the preview. A skill a bot or the Chief asks for (Things that need the owner) stays one skill: such a link is refused there, naming the skills.
- `POST /api/skills/import/confirm` with `{ id, name?, bots? }` imports a one-skill preview under `name` (default the preview's), replacing a skill of that name, gives it to `bots` and answers the skill (`201`).
- `POST /api/skills/import/confirm` with `{ id, skills: [folder], overwrite?, bots? }` imports the skills of a many-skill preview whose `folder` is listed, one after another, each fetched again and written as one import writes it, and gives each to `bots`. One the library has is skipped unless `overwrite: true`; one that fails is said and the rest go on. It answers `201` with `{ imported: [name], skipped: [name], failed: [{ name, error }], text }`, `text` the sentence to show, such as "Imported 30 skills. Skipped 8 you already have.". With no listed folder in the preview it is `400` ("Tick at least one skill to import."). A preview is used once and kept 30 minutes: after that either form is `400` ("This preview expired. Paste the link again.").

## Setup and updates

- `GET /api/setup`: the setup checklist: `claude` (`{ installed, loggedIn, email }`, with `error`, a sentence, when the server could not ask Claude Code or could not read its answer: `loggedIn` says nothing then; the same `error` holds the sentence "Claude Code on this machine is too old for botdot…" when the installed Claude Code cannot read a prompt from a private file: no run and no reply is started until it is updated, and each one asked for fails with that sentence), `docker`, `access`, `timezone`, `bitwarden`, `connectors`, `meaning`, `done`, and, with `chief-name` in `features`, `chiefName` and `chiefNameAsked` (see The Chief's name). `PUT /api/setup` takes `done`, `timezone` and `chiefName`.
- **An Anthropic API key**, when `features` has `claude-api-key` (#35): bots and the Chief can run on the owner's Anthropic API key instead of the Claude subscription. `claude` in `GET /api/setup` (and in every `{ claude }` below) then also has `using`, `"subscription"` or `"api-key"`, which one runs use; `apiKey`, the saved key's last four as `"…wxyz"`, or `null` (the key itself is never answered, logged or shown again); and `subscription`, what the check said of the subscription (`true`, `false`, or `null` when it could not tell). On the key, `loggedIn` is `true` whatever the subscription is, a check of the subscription that could not tell adds no `error` (a Claude Code too old for botdot still does), and the feed's `claude` event says `{ loggedIn: true }`. An app says which one bots run on, and names the other as not used when both are there.
  - `PUT /api/setup/claude/key` with `{ key }` checks the key with Anthropic (a list of one model), keeps it encrypted with the Vault's key, picks it, and returns `{ claude }`. A key that does not start with `sk-ant-`, one Anthropic refuses ("Anthropic refused this API key. Check that you copied all of it.") and one it could not check are `400` with the sentence, and nothing is saved. A new key replaces the old one.
  - `PUT /api/setup/claude/use` with `{ use: "api-key" | "subscription" }` picks which one runs use, keeping the key saved, and returns `{ claude }`; `"api-key"` with no key saved is `400` ("Save an Anthropic API key first.").
  - `DELETE /api/setup/claude/key` removes the key, goes back to the subscription, and returns `{ claude }`.
  - A run or a Chief turn started on the key gets it in `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` and no subscription token; one started on the subscription never gets the key. Runs already going on when the owner switches finish on what they started with. On the key, signing out of the subscription (`DELETE /api/setup/claude`) ends and holds nothing, and answers `stopped: 0`.
- **Sign in to Claude:** `POST /api/setup/claude/login` returns `{ url }`. Open that address in the browser. Then `POST /api/setup/claude/code` with `{ code }` returns `{ claude }`, as in `GET /api/setup`: signed in, or with `error`, a sentence, when the server could not check the sign-in afterwards: an app shows that sentence and does not say "signed in". A code that did not sign in is an error with its sentence.
- **Sign out of Claude:** `DELETE /api/setup/claude` ends every run and reply (`endedBy: "signout"`) and returns `{ claude, stopped }`: `claude` as in `GET /api/setup`, signed out, or with `error` when the server could not check afterwards, which is not "signed out"; `stopped`, how many runs and replies it ended. From the request on the server holds jobs and waiting messages as signed out, until a check says Claude is signed in; when it answers `claude` with `error` and still holds them, it sends the feed's `claude` event with `{ loggedIn: false }` (see A check that could not tell). An error when Claude is still signed in afterwards: nothing is held then, also when the next check cannot tell; `409`, with nothing held or ended, when it is signed in with a token from the server's settings or Claude Code is not installed.
- `GET /api/updates`, `POST /api/updates/check`, and `POST /api/updates/apply` with `{ force? }`. While updating, botdot restarts, so the feed drops, then comes back with `reset`.
- **An account on a machine of several** (ADR 028: `BOT_DOT_ACCOUNT` is set, and it is not the Mac's local botdot server) has no update of its own: the admin updates the front door and every account at once, on the admin page (`POST /admin/update`); its owner may only choose when it switches to the version the admin installed, on the front door's own routes (`/front-door/update`, ADR 053). `features` has no `updates`; `GET /api/updates` and `POST /api/updates/check` answer `{ supported: false, byAdmin: true, state: "idle", available: false, error: null, selfBuilt: false, version }`, and `apply` is `403` "The admin updates botdot for every account at once, on the admin page." The web page, the Android app and the Mac app show no update of botdot then. The Mac's local botdot server keeps its update (`features` has `updates`), which also recreates its front door.
- **A self-built botdot server** (its image is named without a registry, CONTEXT.md), when `features` has `self-built`: the three answer with `selfBuilt: true` and `howToUpdate`, one sentence saying it is updated by pulling the source and building again; `available` stays `false`, a check asks no registry, and `apply` downloads nothing and answers `400` with that sentence as `error`. Every other botdot server has `selfBuilt: false` and no `howToUpdate`. Settings shows the sentence in place of Check and Update.
- **The audit log** has one line `update.applied` for an update that ended healthy, with `actor` `owner` and nothing else. Each removal of the Docker images botdot no longer uses, which on a Linux host follows such an update and a rerun of the installer, writes one line `update.cleaned`, `actor` `owner`, with four whole numbers: `removed`, `skipped` and `failed` (images), and `bytes` (how far Docker's total image size fell; `0` when Docker does not tell). A cleanup that could not run at all is `removed` 0, `skipped` 0, `failed` 1, `bytes` 0; the next update tries again. No response, feed event or screen says any of it.

## Notifications on this device

When `features` has `web-push`, a browser gets the notifications the Android app shows, through Web Push (issue #87): on a desktop browser, Android's Chrome and Firefox, and an iPhone or iPad with botdot added to its Home Screen (iOS 16.4 and later; Safari's tab gets none). The botdot server sends each push itself, to the browser's push service, with the account's own VAPID key (RFC 8292) and the payload encrypted for that browser alone (RFC 8291, `aes128gcm`): no relay, no Apple or Google account. The push service sees whom it is for and how big it is, never what it says. These routes are the owner's alone: the Chief's tools get `403`, and so does anything that did not come through the front door.

- `GET /api/push/key` → `{ publicKey }`: the account's VAPID public key, base64url (the uncompressed P-256 point), for `pushManager.subscribe({ userVisibleOnly: true, applicationServerKey })`. The key pair is made the first time it is asked for and kept in the account's data (`push.json`, readable by botdot alone), so a backup carries it.
- `POST /api/push/subscriptions` with the browser's `subscription.toJSON()` (`{ endpoint, keys: { p256dh, auth } }`) and optionally `device` (a few words: "iPhone, Home Screen") → `{ ok: true }`. The same endpoint again replaces it. `400` for an endpoint that is no https address, or keys that are not what a browser gives. The server keeps the address the browser reached it by (`Host`) as the token's `sub`, so the push service knows where pushes come from.
- `DELETE /api/push/subscriptions` with `{ endpoint }` → `{ ok: true, removed }`. A subscription whose push service answers `404` or `410` is removed by the server itself.
- **What a push says:** JSON `{ title, body, target, tag }`, nothing else: `title` is who it is from (the bot, the Chief's name, "Mail Sorter asks", "Coder in Dev"), `body` the plain text (no Markdown) of at most 300 characters, `target` what a tap opens (`"bot:<id>"`, `"chief:<chatId>"`, `"group:<id>"`, `"needs"` for Needs you, `"app"`), and `tag` the notification it replaces (the chat, or the card's `"job:…"`, `"ask:…"`, `"login:…"`, `"memory:…"`, `"tile:…"`, `"plan:…"`, `"claude"`). No key, token or address of the account is in it.
- **What is sent**, by the rules the Android app follows (see What a notification says, New replies): a `run_finished` or `chief_reply` with `new: true` (so not for a chat on screen in any app, a quiet run, nor a report held in quiet hours: that one is sent when it is delivered, with `new`); a `group_run_finished` with `notify: true`; and once each, by its `tag`, what waits for the owner: a login or payment, a job, a tile, a memory change, an ask of any kind and a bot's plan for the Chief (`chief_suggestion`, not while its chat is on screen), never while the web page is in use (`webActive`), where it shows already, and again only after it was answered and came back; and that Claude is signed out, once per sign-out, also while the web page is in use. What rang is kept over a restart. Bitwarden being locked is not sent. A push has `Urgency: high` for what waits for the owner and `normal` for a reply, and a TTL of a day.
- **The web page** has the switch "Notifications on this device" in Settings. It asks the browser's permission when it is turned on, never on load, registers `/sw.js` (the service worker: it shows notifications and opens their `target`, and keeps `/offline.html` alone, shown for a page that can't be loaded; the page also registers it on every load, issue #163), subscribes and posts the subscription; off, it unsubscribes and deletes it. Each time the page loads, a browser that is subscribed and allowed posts its subscription again. Opening a chat closes that chat's notifications in this browser. In Safari on an iPhone or iPad (`navigator.standalone` false) it says "Add to Home Screen first" in place of the switch.

## The Android app's update

- `GET /api/version` includes `android: { versionCode, versionName, sha256, size }`, the app this botdot's image carries (empty when running from source).
- `GET /api/app/android.apk` downloads it. The app compares `versionCode` with its own, checks `sha256`, and hands the file to Android's installer, which only accepts it when it's signed with the same key.

## The Mac app's update

- `GET /api/version` also includes `mac: { versionCode, versionName, sha256, size, release }`, the Mac app this botdot's image carries (empty when running from source, and in botdot's images until the Mac app is released, [ADR 022](adr/022-the-mac-app-is-developed-again.md)). `release` is the signed manifest described in [release signing](release-signing.md).
- `GET /api/app/mac.zip` downloads the Mac app (a zipped botdot.app). The app verifies the manifest with its pinned publisher key, rejects rollback, checks the downloaded hash and app identity, and replaces itself with the verified app.

## Token usage

When `features` has `token-usage` ([#301](https://github.com/Kordal/bot-dot/issues/301)), Settings has a **Usage** section: the tokens Claude Code reported for this account, for a timeframe the owner picks (Today, 7 days, 30 days, or custom days), in total, per bot and per day. Without the feature the apps show no section. Only the owner reads it: the Chief's tools get `403`. No cost is kept or shown (rule 5 of `AGENTS.md`): tokens only.

- `GET /api/usage?from=YYYY-MM-DD&to=YYYY-MM-DD`: both days included, in the account's time zone. Without `from` and `to`, today; with one of them, that day alone. `400` for a day that is not one, a `to` before `from`, or a range longer than `keepDays`. Answers:
  `{ from, to, today, timeZone, keepDays: 400, totals, bots: [{ id, name, chief?, gone?, …counts }], days: [{ day, …counts }] }`, where the counts (also all of `totals`) are `{ inputTokens, outputTokens, cacheReadTokens, cacheWriteTokens, totalTokens, runs }`. `today` is the account's today, from which the apps count Today, 7 days (`today` and the 6 days before it) and 30 days. `bots` holds every bot that used something in the range, the most tokens first: the Chief of Staff is the row with `chief: true` and `id: null` (its turns, looks, checkups and the hats it picks), and a bot deleted since has `gone: true` and the name it last used tokens under. A group's turn counts for the member whose run it is. `days` has every day of the range, in order, with zeros for a day with nothing. `runs` counts Claude Code's `result` events.
- **How it is kept.** Every `result` event of a bot's run, a group's turn and a Chief turn adds its `usage` (`input_tokens`, `output_tokens`, `cache_read_input_tokens`, `cache_creation_input_tokens`) to `data/usage.json`, one sum per day and bot; its `total_cost_usd` is not kept. 400 days are kept; older days are dropped when something is recorded. Nothing is read back from older runs: usage starts with the update that brought `token-usage`. Each account has its own data folder, so an account sees only its own usage.

## Backups

Only the owner can use these (not the Chief's tools).

- `GET /api/backups` returns `{ configured, running, error, last: { name, size, at } | null, count, keep }`.
- `PUT /api/backups/passphrase` with `{ passphrase }` (at least 8 characters). Backups are made every night at 03:00 once it's set. Changing it only affects new backups.
- `POST /api/backups/now` makes one right away.
- `GET /api/backups/:name` downloads one (`bot-dot-YYYYMMDD-HHMMSS.bdb`), saved as `bot-dot-<account>-YYYYMMDD-HHMMSS.bdb` on a machine of several. **A backup holds one account:** each account's botdot server has its own data and vault key, so its backups hold that account alone, and the manifest names it (`account`). The Android app downloads it with its own session, to the phone's Downloads.
- **Restore:** `POST /api/backups/upload` with the file as the raw body returns `{ file }`. Then `POST /api/backups/restore` with `{ file, passphrase }` returns `{ ok, createdAt, bots, account }` (`account`: the account the backup was made in, `null` for one from before accounts), and botdot restarts with the backup's data. The data it replaces is kept in `data/backups/replaced-<time>`. It's refused while bots are working.

## Not for clients

`/api/self/session/…`, `/api/vault/session/…` and `/api/connectors/session/…` are the bots' own run tools. They only accept a bot's own processes, with that run's key. The three for memory, `POST /api/self/session/memory`, `/recall` and `/run`, are written down under [A bot's own memory](#a-bots-own-memory). `POST /api/connectors/session/run` answers a write that has no grant with `{ ok: false, needsApproval: true, waits: true, requestId }`; the bot's tools then ask `POST /api/connectors/session/wait` with `{ id: requestId }`, a long poll of at most 25 seconds that answers `{ ok: true, status: "waiting" | "allowed" | "declined" | "stopped", text }` for an ask of that same run and `404` for any other, and send `run` again after `allowed` (see An action in a connected app). `POST /api/vault/session/computer/hold` is a bot's `keep_computer_on` (#340): its computer is started if it was not, and stays on after this run ends, until the owner's next message to the bot in its own chat starts a run (the computer goes off when that run ends, unless the bot holds it again), the owner turns it off or resets it (`POST /api/bots/:id/computer` with `action` `stop` or `reset`), the bot's computer setting changes, or 1 hour after the run ended. It answers `{ ok: true, text }`, `text` the sentence for the bot, and `{ ok: false, error }` for a bot without a computer; the apps see nothing new. `POST /api/vault/session/computer/run-with-login` with `{ login, command, timeoutSeconds? }` is a bot's `run_with_login`: it runs `command` (one shell command, at most 4000 characters) with `sh -c` as the bot user on the bot's own computer, with the login's username in `BOTDOT_USERNAME` and its secret in `BOTDOT_PASSWORD` (for a `github-app`, `x-access-token` and an installation token, never its key), in a `docker exec` that takes both from the docker CLI's own environment, so neither is on a command line or in a file. It is refused with `{ ok: false, error }` for a bot without a computer, a login not shared with the bot, and a login whose `inComputer` is off (the error sends the bot to `ask_vault_switch`). `timeoutSeconds` is 120 by default and 600 at most. With `logins` in place of `login` (#228), a list of 1 to 8 logins, each login's username and secret are in `BOTDOT_<NAME>_USERNAME` and `BOTDOT_<NAME>_PASSWORD` instead, `<NAME>` its name in capitals with every character other than A-Z and 0-9 turned into `_` (`OVH_APPLICATION_KEY` gives `BOTDOT_OVH_APPLICATION_KEY_PASSWORD`), and `BOTDOT_USERNAME` and `BOTDOT_PASSWORD` are not set. Every login of the list must pass as a single one does, or no command runs: the error names each one that is not shared or has `inComputer` off, and two names that give the same variable. Both `login` and `logins`, or more than 8, are refused. What comes back has every login's secret blanked out, and the server's log line names each login used, never a value. It answers `{ ok: true, stdout, stderr, exitCode, timedOut?, note? }`, each output cut at 20000 characters, with the secret blanked out as `[hidden]` in every form botdot knows: as it is, URL-encoded, in base64, and as base64 of `username:password`. The bot writes the command, so this stops an accidental leak only: a bot that tries can still get the value. That is why `inComputer` is off until the owner turns it on for one login. The apps see nothing new. `POST /api/vault/session/http` with `{ login, method?, url, headers?, body?, timeoutSeconds? }` is a bot's `api_request`: botdot fills `{{username}}`, `{{password}}` (or `{{token}}`, `{{secret}}`) and `{{basic}}` in the headers with the login's values, sends the request only to the login's address or one of its `allowedHosts`, and answers `{ ok: true, text }`, the status and the response with the secret blanked out, or `{ ok: false, error }`. With `logins` in place of `login` (#228), up to 8, each value is named by its login: `{{NAME.password}}`, `{{NAME.username}}`, `{{NAME.token}}` or `{{NAME.basic}}`, `NAME` a login of the list; a name not in the list, and the unnamed form, are refused. The address must be one that every login of the list allows, and the response has every secret blanked out. Placeholders go in headers only, never in the address or the body, either way. `POST /api/vault/session/computer/run-with-ssh-key` with `{ key, command, timeoutSeconds? }` is a bot's `run_with_ssh_key`: `key` names an SSH key (kind `ssh-key`) of the owner's Bitwarden shared with the bot, and `command` runs as `run-with-login`'s does, with `SSH_AUTH_SOCK` set to an ssh-agent that holds that key, and nothing of the key in its environment. botdot starts the agent as the bot user, loads the key into it as root with its own copy of `deploy/desktop/botdot-ssh-add` (the key on stdin, and only to the agent botdot started, checked by its pid and user), runs the command and ends the agent, so the bot can use the key and cannot read it. It is refused with `{ ok: false, error }` for a bot without a computer, a botdot without Bitwarden, an unknown key, a key not shared with the bot (the error asks the bot to have the owner share it in the Vault), one that asks for the master password again, one without a private key, a computer made before the bot lost root, and one whose agent does not start or whose key does not load (no command runs then). It answers as `run-with-login` does, with the key's text, should it ever come back, blanked out as `[hidden]`. There is no switch like `inComputer`: the bot never has the key.
